A pre-registered test of training on co-occurring versus isolated stressors in a multi-agent fire environment

A pre-registered test of whether training a single policy on co-occurring stressors beats training it on each in isolation, in the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), a multi-agent environment where a fire burns in every episode.

Optional: the correction excludes a large composition effect, not every one (RES-6 upper +0.63 against RES-2 lower +0.47). REQUIRED FACTS, move 4 (what the environment and the method contribute): pure JAX, multi-agent; a cellular-automaton fire in every episode; structural collapse can add ignitions (Coupling A); smoke attenuates perception by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B); an independent communication-denial axis; the fire is an ambient hazard: no reward, cost or constraint channel reads it, and the reward reads task variables and agent deaths and prices every death the same (ENV-1, Def. 2); severity placed relative to a measured percolation threshold (CAL-1, CAL-3); each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel, so ablations are bitwise-exact nested models (N9); "we describe" a measurement protocol with reproducibility floors per metric, per GPU and per artifact (METH-1, METH-2), never "every bar is graded against a floor". Optional, if the budget allows (Q5): collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-fold from low to high severity under trained policies (CA-4), a fall the random-policy calibration had already shown before any trained policy was measured (DX-9), never "as predicted before measurement" (OI-1). We report a pre-registered test of whether training one policy on co-occurring stressors beats training it on each in isolation, in the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), a pure-JAX multi-agent environment where a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode.1 Two stressors are coupled to the fire: structural collapse can add ignitions, and smoke attenuates perception by Beer–Lambert transmittance; a third, communication denial, is independent. Joint and isolated training are run at matched compute and evaluated at a held-out near-critical severity, with task completion and survival as Šidák-corrected co-primaries. We ran 72DES-3 seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60DES-1, a recorded deviation made before unblinding.

Joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [+0.47, +1.44]RES-2); completion shows no detectable change (−1.73RES-1 points, CI [−4.12, +0.66]RES-1), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66RES-1 points. Both differences are sign unstable over the final halfRES-11 of training: the survival gap opens only in the last fifthRES-12, and completion leans negative from 70 %RES-13 of the budget. The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. Communication denial, inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale, is the only stressor in a thirdDES-8 of the isolated arm's training episodes, which are thus fire-only in effect. This is our design error: we kept a stressor already found inert and did not count the fire-only share it created; found before the main training runs, the bias was registered with its correction while they ran, before unblinding. The correction is a 20DES-13DES-15-seed sensitivity arm without those episodes, outside the two confirmatory tests; joint training's survival differs from that arm's by −0.03RES-6 points (Šidák-corrected CI [−0.68, +0.63]RES-6), though it still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often, always combined.

The fire is an ambient hazard that no reward, cost or constraint channel reads; the reward reads only task variables and agent deaths, pricing every death the same. Severity is set relative to a measured percolation threshold; each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel, and switching it off reproduces the nested model's trajectories bit for bit. We describe a measurement protocol with reproducibility floors per metric, per GPU and per artifact. We recommend, from this single environment, that studies of compositional generalization match the share of training with an active stressor before attributing a gain to composition.

We report a pre-registered test of whether training one policy on co-occurring stressors beats training it on each in isolation, in the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), a pure-JAX multi-agent environment where a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode.<d-footnote>We used the LLM assistant Claude under our direction; its uses included drafting design documents and reviews, proposing options for our decisions, writing and running code and analyses, making figures, helping with literature search and citations, and drafting this text from our records. The authors are responsible for every word.</d-footnote> Two stressors are coupled to the fire: structural collapse can add ignitions, and smoke attenuates perception by Beer–Lambert transmittance; a third, communication denial, is independent. Joint and isolated training are run at matched compute and evaluated at a held-out near-critical severity, with task completion and survival as Šidák-corrected co-primaries. We ran 72<!-- N:DES-3 --> seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60<!-- N:DES-1 -->, a recorded deviation made before unblinding.

Joint training raises survival by 0.96<!-- N:RES-2 --> points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [+0.47, +1.44]<!-- N:RES-2 -->); completion shows no detectable change (−1.73<!-- N:RES-1 --> points, CI [−4.12, +0.66]<!-- N:RES-1 -->), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66<!-- N:RES-1 --> points. Both differences are sign unstable over the final half<!-- N:RES-11 --> of training: the survival gap opens only in the last fifth<!-- N:RES-12 -->, and completion leans negative from 70 %<!-- N:RES-13 --> of the budget. The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. Communication denial, inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale, is the only stressor in a third<!-- N:DES-8 --> of the isolated arm's training episodes, which are thus fire-only in effect. This is our design error: we kept a stressor already found inert and did not count the fire-only share it created; found before the main training runs, the bias was registered with its correction while they ran, before unblinding. The correction is a 20<!-- N:DES-13 --><!-- N:DES-15 -->-seed sensitivity arm without those episodes, outside the two confirmatory tests; joint training's survival differs from that arm's by −0.03<!-- N:RES-6 --> points (Šidák-corrected CI [−0.68, +0.63]<!-- N:RES-6 -->), though it still sees each coupling twice<!-- N:DES-14 --> as often, always combined.

We recommend, from this single environment, that studies of compositional generalization match the share of training with an active stressor before attributing a gain to composition.

  <figcaption><strong>Figure 1.</strong> One episode of CHE. The map is environment state: fire, the smoke field ρ, weak and collapsed floor, food, agents alive and dead, and ignitions seeded by collapse. Only the selected agent's three panels concern perception: its observation as produced (labelled obs v3, the masking observation), those cells' true state, for comparison, and P(seen) = τ, the transmittance through smoke. The held-out configuration θ&#42;, episode key, policy and stochastic actions were fixed before rendering<!-- N:ENV-13 -->. <span class="figure-note">Status: illustrative · random policy · grades nothing · Interact: play, scrub, jump to events, toggle layers, pick agents.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

Captions carry a status. Recurring statuses: illustrative (grades nothing); registered (read by a rule fixed before unblinding); sensitivity arm (outside the two confirmatory tests); secondary (carries no verdict); descriptive (no test); reference scale (cross-artifact), a spread measured on other runs, shown for scale only.

DEMO KEEPS (sections/01_introduction.md): the order; Figures 2 and 3 (Γ never without Γ₄ near the opening, FIGURE BATCH A1); the corrected Prop. 1 sentence; the corrected fuel-limitation timing; the two limits of the correction in one clause pointing to section 9.2; the search caveat. CHANGES: the result paragraphs lead with the plain result; Remark 1's clause (C2); the δ paragraph becomes an owned design error with the timeline (F3); "The argument is general …" becomes the confound's logic only (F4); CB-9's number leaves contribution 2 and Medium siting becomes a limitation clause; Figure 2's caption loses its numbers. DROPS: "The fire-only third was registered as a bias"; "a rule whose argument is general"; repeated tags (the k = 72 detail, DES-14's shares beyond one sentence); figure-note boilerplate. NOT HERE: composition effect; "composition does not matter"; budget-robust; "first" or "novel" without the search caveat; "swarm"; PBT; the founding hypothesis as partly supported; ISO-4 against ISO as significant; "exposure" for the active share; "the secondary design attributes"; the recommendation as a contribution or after "therefore". DEPENDENCIES: section 2 relies on this section stating what the comparison holds fixed and the threefold frequency; section 9.2 delivers γ_n and where the plane fit fails (§1 INTRODUCTION item 3), and this section points there; section 10 item 1 carries Medium siting; section 9.1 carries the full F3 account; section 9.3 the recommendation. V6-1 is W6's row.

When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors [1, 2], and climate science the risk from compound events [3, 4]. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization [5]: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) [6] studied it for imitation-learned manipulation; we study it in multi-agent reinforcement learning, for survival and task completion under a lethal fire.

Where the hazard sits in the learning problem shapes what such a comparison can teach: in some fire-related work it is the task [7], and safe reinforcement learning typically places it in a cost or a constraint [8]. In the Compound Hostile Environment (CHE) the fire is an ambient hazard (Definition 2 in §3): a fixed stochastic process that optimizes against no one and enters no reward, cost or constraint channel. The reward reads task variables and agent deaths, and prices every death at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause. No reward or cost signal mentions the hazard in either arm; the agents meet the fire only through what they observe and what happens to them. Proposition 1 shows that the fire, which looks like nonstationarity to any one agent, is part of the state: CHE is a well-defined Dec-POMDP whose states reachable within the horizon form a finite set (§3).

CHE is written in pure JAX, and a cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode (Figure 1). For the idealized kernel the burnt region is exactly a bond-percolation cluster (Proposition 2) [9, 10], and the threeCAL-3 severities place the fire's transmissibility β below, near and above a measured percolation threshold of the implemented kernel (§4). Two stressors are coupled to the fire: collapse can add ignitions (Coupling A, of strength \(\kappa_A\); §5), and smoke attenuates each agent's egocentric observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B, \(\kappa_B\); §6). A third, link denial at level δ, is independent of it (§7); \(\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\) is the environment configuration.

The training question becomes a pre-registered test of two training mixtures (Figure 2).

An isolated arm (ISO) trains one policy on single-stressor configurations and a joint arm (JOINT) one on configurations with every stressor on, both at β = 0.43CAL-3 and 0.70CAL-3. Both are evaluated at θ*, every stressor on at the near-critical β = 0.49CAL-3, which neither saw. Architecture, hyperparameters, evaluation draw and compute (1000DES-13 updates) are matched, with one shared-parameter IPPO policy per training seedENV-11 [11]. The estimand is the compositional gap Γ, JOINT's mean minus ISO's at θ*, on two co-primary outcomes, task completion and survival, Šidák-corrected for m = 2DES-13 tests. FourDES-12 outcome branches, a framing for each and a falsifier were registered on 2026-08-13DES-12, before the grid began (a fifthDX-102 was added before unblinding, the first computation of any outcome contrast; Appendix E). We ran 72DES-3 seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60DES-1, a recorded deviation made before unblinding (§9.1).

JOINT sees each coupling three timesDES-7 as often as ISO, so the endpoints differ in frequency as well as in co-occurrence. A secondary sweep in the spirit of DBCA [12] varies co-occurrence at fixed frequency, but its fire-only share rises with co-occurrenceDES-9, and it bears no verdict (§9.1). Communication denial is inert under our frozen message encoder at this scale (§7), so the composition tested is that of the two couplings, and the thirdDES-8 of ISO's training episodes that carry only δ are fire-only. ISO's active share, the fraction of training episodes with a behaviourally active stressor, is therefore two thirdsDES-14, and JOINT's is oneDES-14. This is an error in our design: it left δ out of the secondary sweeps because δ was inert, yet kept it in ISO's mixture, and did not count the fire-only episodes that resulted. A review of the registered design identified the bias on 2026-08-16V6-1, before the grid; we registered it, its direction, and a correcting arm, ISO-4, with its reading rule on 2026-09-18DES-15, mid-grid and before unblinding (§9.1). At the test's severity Coupling B was behaviourally quiet in calibration: its own survival effect was within the spread over threeCB-9 seeds (§6.4), and none of the threeCAL-3 severities has both couplings strongly live (§10).

At θ*, joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [+0.47, +1.44]RES-2; points are rate differences × 100), and the survival test rejects (Figure 3). Task completion shows no detectable change, −1.73RES-1 points (CI [−4.12, +0.66]RES-1), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66RES-1 points. The founding hypothesis, that joint training improves both outcomes, did not occur. The falsifier registered for an asymmetric outcome passes (§9.2); under Definition 2 the asymmetry lies between a penalized channel, deaths, and a rewarded one, the task. Both co-primaries are sign unstable over the final halfRES-11 of training: the survival gap opens only in the last fifthRES-12 of the budget, and completion leans negative from 70 %RES-13 of it onward (Figure 12).

The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. ISO-4, ISO without its δ-only components, is a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family, trained with 20DES-13 seeds on a second GPUHW-4; its survival is 0.98RES-7 points above ISO's, an estimate with no registered test. The corrected gap Γ₄, JOINT − ISO-4, is −0.03RES-6 points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [−0.68, +0.63]RES-6), although JOINT still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often as ISO-4 and always in combination. A same-GPU replicate, proposed after Γ₄ was seen, agrees. The correction excludes only a large composition effect, and ISO-4 changes the active share and each coupling's frequency together; §9.2 states both limits, what the secondary design adds and where its fit fails.

A joint-versus-isolated comparison that leaves the active share free can report an active-share effect as a composition effect (§9.3). In the search described in §2 we found no reinforcement-learning comparison that trains its isolated arm on a mixture of single stressors and matches that arm to the joint one on this share. We claim no convergence guarantee and no survival gap robust to the training budget: every comparison is at matched compute. Nor do we claim a theorem that joint training wins; Proposition 4 (§9.1), a transfer bound for a fixed policy, bounds only what isolated training can be guaranteed to transfer.

Our contributions are:

  1. CHE, an environment built as an instrument, with severity by measured dynamical phase, bitwise-exact nested ablations and theory that doubles as unit tests (§3–§7).
  2. Calibration findings that stand without the training comparison. Under the calibration's trained policies, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-foldCA-4 from low to high severity, a fall the random-policy calibration had already shown before any trained policy was measured (§5). Smoke costs survival at high severity, in the same direction in three of threeCB-5 replicates (§6), and communication denial is inert under the frozen encoder (§7). Under the measurement protocol of §8, acceptance bars come with floors measured per metric, per GPU and per artifact, and the bars that lack one are named.
  3. A pre-registered test of joint against isolated training (§9), whose survival advantage does not survive the inert-share correction, which was registered before unblinding.

At the test's severity Coupling B was behaviourally quiet in calibration: its own survival effect was within the spread over three<!-- N:CB-9 --> seeds (§6.4), and none of the three<!-- N:CAL-3 --> severities has both couplings strongly live (§10).

<figure class="l-page">
  <iframe src="{{ 'assets/html/submission/opening_ladder.html' | relative_url }}" width="100%" height="427" style="border:none" title="Registered readings: Γ and Γ₄ on survival and completion" loading="lazy"></iframe>
  <figcaption><strong>Figure 3.</strong> Survival: Γ REJECT; the gain “does not survive the inert-share correction” (Γ₄ null). Completion: Γ null; Γ₄ null, a second exclusion bound. Γ is JOINT − ISO, the registered test; Γ₄, the corrected gap, is JOINT − ISO-4, where ISO-4 is a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family. X₄, on the completion row, is Γ₄'s minimum detectable effect at 80 %<!-- N:METH-7 --> power. The text quotes the printed rate differences × 100, as points. On the figure “unit 1” is GPU 1, “cross-unit” means across GPUs, and “full ladder: §9.2” points to Figure 11. The figure cannot show how Γ moved during training (sign unstable over the final half; Figure 12). <span class="figure-note">Status: registered for Γ · sensitivity arm for Γ₄ · Interact: none.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div class="print-companion">
{% include figure.html path="assets/img/submission/opening_ladder.png" class="img-fluid" alt="Joint minus isolated training and joint minus ISO-4, for survival and completion, each with its Šidák-corrected interval and registered verdict; the survival gain is shown beside the ISO-4 contrast, labelled does not survive the inert-share correction." caption="Static companion of Figure 3 for the printed PDF." %}
</div>

At θ\*, joint training raises survival by 0.96<!-- N:RES-2 --> points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [+0.47, +1.44]<!-- N:RES-2 -->; points are rate differences × 100), and the survival test rejects (Figure 3). Task completion shows no detectable change, −1.73<!-- N:RES-1 --> points (CI [−4.12, +0.66]<!-- N:RES-1 -->), which excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66<!-- N:RES-1 --> points. The founding hypothesis, that joint training improves both outcomes, did not occur. The falsifier registered for an asymmetric outcome passes (§9.2); under Definition 2 the asymmetry lies between a penalized channel, deaths, and a rewarded one, the task. Both co-primaries are sign unstable over the final half<!-- N:RES-11 --> of training: the survival gap opens only in the last fifth<!-- N:RES-12 --> of the budget, and completion leans negative from 70 %<!-- N:RES-13 --> of it onward (Figure 12).

The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. ISO-4, ISO without its δ-only components, is a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family, trained with 20<!-- N:DES-13 --> seeds on a second GPU<!-- N:HW-4 -->; its survival is 0.98<!-- N:RES-7 --> points above ISO's, an estimate with no registered test. The corrected gap Γ₄, JOINT − ISO-4, is −0.03<!-- N:RES-6 --> points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [−0.68, +0.63]<!-- N:RES-6 -->), although JOINT still sees each coupling twice<!-- N:DES-14 --> as often as ISO-4 and always in combination. A same-GPU replicate, proposed after Γ₄ was seen, agrees. The correction excludes only a large composition effect, and ISO-4 changes the active share and each coupling's frequency together; §9.2 states both limits, what the secondary design adds and where its fit fails.

A joint-versus-isolated comparison that leaves the active share free can report an active-share effect as a composition effect (§9.3). In the search described in §2 we found no reinforcement-learning comparison that trains its isolated arm on a mixture of single stressors and matches that arm to the joint one on this share. We claim no convergence guarantee and no survival gap robust to the training budget: every comparison is at matched compute. Nor do we claim a theorem that joint training wins; Proposition 4 (§9.1), a transfer bound for a fixed policy, bounds only what isolated training can be guaranteed to transfer.

Our contributions are:

1. CHE, an environment built as an instrument, with severity by measured dynamical phase, bitwise-exact nested ablations and theory that doubles as unit tests (§3–§7).
2. Calibration findings that stand without the training comparison. Under the calibration's trained policies, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> from low to high severity, a fall the random-policy calibration had already shown before any trained policy was measured (§5). Smoke costs survival at high severity, in the same direction in three of three<!-- N:CB-5 --> replicates (§6), and communication denial is inert under the frozen encoder (§7). Under the measurement protocol of §8, acceptance bars come with floors measured per metric, per GPU and per artifact, and the bars that lack one are named.
3. A pre-registered test of joint against isolated training (§9), whose survival advantage does not survive the inert-share correction, which was registered before unblinding.

2 Related work

Table 1 sets the nearest fire-related and safety-related work beside CHE.

Table 1. Where the hazard enters the learning or planning problem.

work agents and method where the hazard enters
Haksar & Schwager (2018) [7] aerial agents fighting wildfire; multi-agent deep Q-network the task: a reward for moving to burning trees on the fire's boundary (their Algorithm 2); the paper gives the fire no way to harm the agents
JaxWildfire (Çakır et al., 2025) [13] one air-tanker agent; JAX cellular-automaton wildfire simulator the reward, hazard-priced: a per-step penalty proportional to the number of burning cells, with a bonus for full extinction; harm to the agent is not described
safe reinforcement learning, as García & Fernández (2015) define it [8] learning policies that maximize return while ensuring reasonable system performance and/or respecting safety constraints a constraint or a cost, typically
VULCAN (Liu & Yan, 2026) [14] a proposed approach to multi-robot fire search and rescue, planned by a vision-language model rather than learned a hazard cost map that folds in smoke, heat and sensing uncertainty
CHE (this paper) 12ENV-6 agents; independent PPO ambient: the transition and observation kernels only; no reward, cost or constraint reads the fire, and the reward prices each death at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause

CHE's reward is hazard-blind, while JaxWildfire's is hazard-priced: ours prices an outcome, each death the same (Definition 2 in §3.3). VULCAN's preliminary evaluation covers baselines only, and its evaluation metric, cumulative hazard exposure, is also abbreviated CHE, unrelated to our environment's name.

Coupling A lets structural collapse add ignitions. The nearest neighbour we found is Raut et al. (2026) [15], who model public-safety power shutoffs as a single-agent decision problem. In it, the power flows that follow from the operator's switching decisions set the failure probability of lines in wildfire areas, which the authors treat as ignition risk. Their reward is negative operating cost, and there is no spatial spread model. CHE's agents influence the hazard too, but weakly and indirectly: an agent standing on a weak cell raises that cell's collapse rate, yet most collapses happen with no agent present (§5.1). A collapse may then seed fire, so we do not claim that CHE's hazard grows without agent influence. The difference from Raut et al. is one of directness and size: there the controlled decision sets the failure probability, while in CHE ignition is not an action available to the agents.

Coupling B attenuates each agent's observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance along the line of sight through smoke. VULCAN degrades simulated sensing with smoke density and visibility loss, and infers smoke density from image degradation and depth consistency, but it has no transmittance model. JaxWildfire's observations are partial and egocentric but not attenuated. In the search described below, we found no reinforcement-learning environment whose observation kernel attenuates by transmittance.

CHE follows JaxMARL's environment conventions [16] and trains with independent PPO [11]. Among the JAX multi-agent benchmarks we surveyed, JaxMARL, Multi-Agent Craftax [17] and Assistax [18], none has an evolving, spreading lethal hazard field or perception attenuated by a hazard. Multi-Agent Craftax comes closest in kind: its agents lose health to hunger and enemies and can die, but its lava is static terrain that agents cannot enter. In Crafter and in Craftax-Classic, the JAX reimplementation described alongside Craftax, lava is lethal, and Crafter and Craftax reward each health point regained and penalize each one lost [19, 20], pricing an outcome, as our reward does. POBAX [21] is a single-agent suite for partial observability, and BenchMARL [22] a multi-agent benchmarking library built on TorchRL.

Domain randomization trains on a broad, fixed distribution over simulator parameters so that the deployed setting looks like one more sample [23, 24]. Unsupervised environment design adapts the distribution to the learner instead. PAIRED generates environments from a regret objective [25]; Prioritized Level Replay revisits levels by their estimated learning potential [26] and was later recast as environment design [27]; and ACCEL evolves levels near the frontier of the agent's ability [28]. These methods ask which distribution to train on, usually adaptively; we compare two fixed distributions and evaluate no adaptive curriculum (§10).

The closest earlier study of our question is Gao et al. (2024) [6]. In visual imitation learning for robotic manipulation, they ask whether policies compose environmental factors, such as object type and position, table texture and height, and camera position, to succeed on unseen combinations. They compare data-collection strategies at a matched number of demonstrations, among them varying one factor at a time and cycling through factors so that more combinations appear (their §III-C and §IV-A). Policies do compose, more weakly when both factors are physical (their §V-B), and the strategy that cycles through factors is generally the most effective, which the authors hypothesize is due to the combinations it covers. Their design is built for data efficiency and does not separate co-occurrence from how often each factor value appears. CompoSuite [29] studies the composition of tasks, over robots, objects, objectives and obstacles, in multi-task reinforcement learning. The DBCA method [12] builds splits whose atom distributions are similar and whose compound distributions differ. Our secondary sweep follows that idea, holding each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies; our ISO and JOINT endpoints do not, since JOINT sees each coupling three timesDES-7 as often as ISO (§1, §9.1).

Robust reinforcement learning asks the same question about perturbations. Erdem & Üre (2025) [30] train single-agent continuous-control policies against a learned adversary that perturbs observations, actions, or both, and report that agents trained against one modality remain vulnerable to simultaneous attacks on both (their Tables 3–4). Under single-type attacks, agents trained against their balanced mixed adversary do at least as well as those trained against a naive one in all six cells, one a tie (their Table 5). Of their six training arms, one is an unattacked baseline; in each of the others an attack is computed at every step (their Algorithm 1). No attacked arm schedules clean steps, so none has a counterpart of the fire-only share our correction removes. No arm trains on a mixture of single-type attacks, and the mixed arms carry the combined budget, which the authors note is more severe. Their design thus bundles per-modality frequency, co-occurrence and a larger budget, which is nearer to what our corrected contrast leaves in, where JOINT still sees each coupling twiceDES-14 as often as ISO-4; it neither supports nor contradicts our corrected null. Agrawal et al. (2023) [31] train multi-agent policies against random reward, state and action noise, one or two types at a time, under a curriculum that raises the noise level and sets training length, so exposure differs between their models. In supervised vision, Tramèr & Boneh (2019) [32] and Hsiung et al. (2023) [33] study robustness to multiple and to composite perturbations.

Chen et al. (2022) [34] model domain randomization as returning the optimal history-dependent policy of a latent MDP, a set of fully observed MDPs that differ in their transitions. Every upper bound they prove on its shortfall assumes each MDP is communicating, with bounded diameter. Theorem 1 (§6) is kin to the construction of their Proposition 1, in which an irreversible choice is made before anything distinguishes the candidate MDPs, but that construction does not cover Theorem 1. That construction obtains a shortfall growing linearly in the horizon from per-step rewards in absorbing states, while Theorem 1's two-corridor toy has a single terminal reward and can reveal the corridor before the branch. Read as a latent MDP with the signals encoded as states, the toy has an absorbing failure, so their upper bounds do not apply either. Theorem 1 also measures a different gap: the adaptive optimum against the policy optimal for a fixed map. Chen et al. therefore do not subsume Theorem 1, and Theorem 1 does not extend their bounds. Pynadath & Tambe (2002) [35] relate the complexity of team decision problems with communication to observability. Free communication lowers the complexity when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ (§7) refines this for collective partial observability: communication's marginal value vanishes when interchangeable agents' views are redundant, and returns when roles are irreplaceable or deaths are costly. A survey of adaptability in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Hu et al., 2025, revised 2026) [36] calls an environment family highly diagnostic if it "systematically varies" factors such as population, observability or communication structure "while holding other factors fixed". It mentions compositional generalization but does not analyse shifts in several factors at once.

We ran the literature search with the help of LLM-based research assistants, between August and October 2026DX-101. A census of benchmark venues and a search for theorems that could subsume ours came first; a search of domain randomization, environment design, multi-task and robust-learning neighbours followed. Both used web search and the arXiv API and read abstracts. The works this section describes in detail were then read in full; the remaining citations rest on their abstracts. We did not query IEEE Xplore. Wherever this paper says that no work does something, it means no work found by this search.

Our secondary sweep follows that idea, holding each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies; our ISO and JOINT endpoints do not, since JOINT sees each coupling three times<!-- N:DES-7 --> as often as ISO (§1, §9.1).

Robust reinforcement learning asks the same question about perturbations. Erdem & Üre (2025) <d-cite key="erdem2025mixed"></d-cite> train single-agent continuous-control policies against a learned adversary that perturbs observations, actions, or both, and report that agents trained against one modality remain vulnerable to simultaneous attacks on both (their Tables 3–4). Under single-type attacks, agents trained against their balanced mixed adversary do at least as well as those trained against a naive one in all six cells, one a tie (their Table 5). Of their six training arms, one is an unattacked baseline; in each of the others an attack is computed at every step (their Algorithm 1). No attacked arm schedules clean steps, so none has a counterpart of the fire-only share our correction removes. No arm trains on a mixture of single-type attacks, and the mixed arms carry the combined budget, which the authors note is more severe. Their design thus bundles per-modality frequency, co-occurrence and a larger budget, which is nearer to what our corrected contrast leaves in, where JOINT still sees each coupling twice<!-- N:DES-14 --> as often as ISO-4; it neither supports nor contradicts our corrected null. Agrawal et al. (2023) <d-cite key="agrawal2023multimodal"></d-cite> train multi-agent policies against random reward, state and action noise, one or two types at a time, under a curriculum that raises the noise level and sets training length, so exposure differs between their models. In supervised vision, Tramèr & Boneh (2019) <d-cite key="tramer2019multiple"></d-cite> and Hsiung et al. (2023) <d-cite key="hsiung2023compositional"></d-cite> study robustness to multiple and to composite perturbations.

Chen et al. (2022) <d-cite key="chen2022understandingdr"></d-cite> model domain randomization as returning the optimal history-dependent policy of a latent MDP, a set of fully observed MDPs that differ in their transitions. Every upper bound they prove on its shortfall assumes each MDP is communicating, with bounded diameter. Theorem 1 (§6) is kin to the construction of their Proposition 1, in which an irreversible choice is made before anything distinguishes the candidate MDPs, but that construction does not cover Theorem 1. That construction obtains a shortfall growing linearly in the horizon from per-step rewards in absorbing states, while Theorem 1's two-corridor toy has a single terminal reward and can reveal the corridor before the branch. Read as a latent MDP with the signals encoded as states, the toy has an absorbing failure, so their upper bounds do not apply either. Theorem 1 also measures a different gap: the adaptive optimum against the policy optimal for a fixed map. Chen et al. therefore do not subsume Theorem 1, and Theorem 1 does not extend their bounds. Pynadath & Tambe (2002) <d-cite key="pynadath2002"></d-cite> relate the complexity of team decision problems with communication to observability. Free communication lowers the complexity when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ (§7) refines this for collective partial observability: communication's marginal value vanishes when interchangeable agents' views are redundant, and returns when roles are irreplaceable or deaths are costly. A survey of adaptability in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Hu et al., 2025, revised 2026) <d-cite key="hu2025adaptablemarl"></d-cite> calls an environment family highly diagnostic if it "systematically varies" factors such as population, observability or communication structure "while holding other factors fixed". It mentions compositional generalization but does not analyse shifts in several factors at once.

We ran the literature search with the help of LLM-based research assistants, between August and October 2026<!-- N:DX-101 -->. A census of benchmark venues and a search for theorems that could subsume ours came first; a search of domain randomization, environment design, multi-task and robust-learning neighbours followed. Both used web search and the arXiv API and read abstracts. The works this section describes in detail were then read in full; the remaining citations rest on their abstracts. We did not query IEEE Xplore. Wherever this paper says that no work does something, it means no work found by this search.

CHE is a grid world in which a team of agents forages while a fire burns; Figure 4 shows one step.

Collapse, fire, smoke, agents and communication are sampled in the order of Proposition 1. Coupling A (orange) carries c′ − c into the fire; Coupling B (blue) carries ρ′ into the observation, drawn from s′. The comms kernel takes δ and reads no hazard; TX reads the post-step h′ and c′, as in the code. Arrow colours are this figure's own. The reward is not drawn (Definition 2). Status: schematic, no data.

3.1 State, kernel and step order

Definition 1 (Compound Hostile Environment). A CHE is a Dec-POMDP \(\mathcal{M}_\theta = \langle I, \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}, T_\theta, R, \{\Omega_i\}, O_\theta, \gamma \rangle\) with a finite set of agents \(I\) on a grid \(G\), action sets \(\mathcal{A}_i\), observation sets \(\Omega_i\), reward \(R\) (Definition 2), observation kernel \(O_\theta\) and discount \(\gamma\). It is indexed by the environment configuration \(\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\): the fire's per-neighbour ignition probability \(\beta\), the strengths \(\kappa_A\) and \(\kappa_B\) of Couplings A and B, and the communication-denial level \(\delta\). Its state space factors as

\[\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{H} \times \mathcal{P} \times \mathcal{C} \times \mathcal{K} \times \{0, \dots, T\},\]

where \(x \in \mathcal{X}\) holds the agents' positions \(x_i \in G\), their alive flags \(\alpha_i \in \{0, 1\}\) and the task state; \(h \in \mathcal{H} = \{\mathrm{Fuel}, \mathrm{Burning}, \mathrm{Burnt}\}^G\) is the fire; \(\rho \in \mathcal{P} = \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^G\) is the smoke field; \(c \in \mathcal{C} = \{\mathrm{intact}, \mathrm{collapsed}\}^G\) is the structure; \(k \in \mathcal{K}\) is the realized communication graph on \(I\); and the last factor is the step index \(t\), with horizon \(T\). The transition kernel factorizes as

\[\begin{aligned} T_\theta(s' \mid s, a) = {} & \underbrace{T_C(c' \mid c, x)}_{\mathrm{collapse}} \; \underbrace{T_H^{\beta,\kappa_A}(h' \mid h, c, c')}_{\mathrm{fire;\ Coupling\ A}} \; \underbrace{T_\rho(\rho' \mid \rho, h')}_{\mathrm{smoke}} \\ & \times \underbrace{T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')}_{\mathrm{agents}} \; \underbrace{T_K^{\delta}(k' \mid x')}_{\mathrm{comms}}, \end{aligned}\]

and agent \(i\)'s observation is drawn from the post-step state, \(o'_i \sim O^i_{\kappa_B}(\cdot \mid x', h', \rho', c', k')\). The dependence of \(T_H\) on the collapse increment \(c' - c\) is Coupling A (§5); the dependence of \(O^i_{\kappa_B}\) on the fire, through the smoke field \(\rho'\) it produces, is Coupling B (§6). A training mixture (§9.1) draws \(\theta\) once per episode, at reset. The slopes \(\gamma_p\) and \(\gamma_n\) (§9.2, Appendix H) are unrelated to this discount.

Proposition 1 (Well-posedness and Markov closure). For every \(\theta\), the process \(s_t = (x_t, h_t, \rho_t, c_t, k_t, t)\) under any joint policy is a Markov chain, and \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\) is a well-defined Dec-POMDP: finite-horizon optimal joint policies exist, and the standard value definitions apply.

The proof (Appendix A) composes the five kernels in order. Because \(\rho\) is real-valued the state space is not finite, but the states and observations reachable within the horizon form finite sets, and restricted to them \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\) is a finite Dec-POMDP.

Remark 1 (Hardness). For two or more agents, the finite-horizon Dec-POMDP decision problem is NEXP-hard, and NEXP-complete when the horizon is below the number of states (Bernstein et al., 2002)[37]. This bounds the worst case over the class and says nothing about a particular instance; it licenses the expectation that no general exact planner will scale, which is why we learn policies rather than plan.

Listing 1, the body of step, follows the factorization; the link graph is drawn on its own stream with no hazard argument, and the reward line reads only the task reward and the two death counts. In the listing, tl is the episode's \(\theta\), and no configuration here uses the early frozen control branch. The observation is then drawn from the new state on its own stream (che/env/env.py:394-396).

Listing 1. One environment step in the order of Proposition 1.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/env.py:298-358
# che/env/env.py:298-358 (verbatim)
    k_struct, k_seed, k_fire = jax.random.split(key, 3)

    # 1. c' ~ T_C(c, x): reads *pre-step* occupancy.
    occ_pre = occupancy_grid(state.agent_pos, state.agent_alive, cfg.grid_size)
    structure_new = structure_step(
        k_struct,
        state.structure,
        state.weak,
        occ_pre.astype(jnp.float32),
        lambda_0=th.lambda_0,
        lambda_load=th.lambda_load,
    )
    collapse_increment = (structure_new == COLLAPSED) & (state.structure == INTACT)

    # 2. h' ~ T_H(h, c, c'): CA spread, then the Coupling A impulse from the
    # collapse increment (seeded cells are Burning in h', spread next step).
    seed_mask = coupling_a_seed_mask(
        k_seed, collapse_increment, kappa_A=tl.kappa_A, r_seed=th.r_seed
    )
    hazard_ca = hazard_step(k_fire, state.hazard, beta=tl.beta, iota=th.iota)
    if cfg.hazard_mode == "frozen":
        # M1.3: h is frozen — the CA/seed draws above still happen and are
        # discarded, so dynamic<->frozen with the same key share every
        # other stream bitwise (invariant #3). Frozen Burning cells stay
        # Burning: lethal and smoke-emitting, but never spread or burn out.
        hazard_new = state.hazard
        seeded_ignitions = jnp.zeros_like(seed_mask)
    else:
        hazard_new = seed_ignitions(hazard_ca, seed_mask)
        seeded_ignitions = hazard_ca != hazard_new  # Fuel cells Coupling A lit

    # 3. rho' from h' (Def. 6; smoke outlives flame).
    smoke_new = smoke_step(state.smoke, hazard_new, sigma_s=th.sigma_s, eta=th.eta)

    # 4. x' ~ T_X + task dynamics. M1.1: lethality/blocking against h'/c'.
    # DECISION: an agent disabled this step does not collect food this step
    # (it died before/on arrival) — occupancy filters on the post-death
    # alive vector, keeping "dead agents never collect" exact.
    pos_new, alive_new, deaths_fire, deaths_collapse, blocked_moves = agent_step(
        state.agent_pos,
        state.agent_alive,
        actions,
        hazard_new,
        structure_new,
        collapse_increment,
        cfg.grid_size,
    )
    occ_post = occupancy_grid(pos_new, alive_new, cfg.grid_size)
    food_new, task_reward = task_step(state.food, occ_post)
    # Def.-2-compliant death penalty: reads only the alpha transition (an X
    # variable) — never hazard/smoke/structure directly.
    reward = task_reward - th.death_penalty * (deaths_fire + deaths_collapse).astype(
        jnp.float32
    )

    # 5. k' ~ T_K(x'): comms channel (Def. 7, M5.0). Reads post-step
    # positions/aliveness only — never h', rho' or c' — and draws from its
    # own fold_in stream, so delta cannot perturb any kernel above.
    links, links_in_range = _comms_obs(
        jax.random.fold_in(key, _COMMS_STREAM), pos_new, alive_new, cfg, tl.delta
    )

3.2 The fire and its smoke

Definition 3 (Fire kernel). Each cell is Fuel, Burning or Burnt. At each step, independently for each ordered pair of a Burning cell \(g\) and a Fuel von Neumann neighbour \(g' \in N(g)\), the cell \(g'\) ignites with probability \(\beta\). A Burning cell becomes Burnt after exactly one step, and Burnt is absorbing. Fuel cells may also ignite spontaneously at rate \(\iota \ge 0\) per step; CHE uses \(\iota = 0\)DX-1.

Every episode starts from an all-Fuel grid with oneV3-1 Burning cell at a uniformly random position (che/env/env.py:241-243). One uniform per cell and incoming direction realizes the per-edge Bernoulli variables of Proposition 2's coupling exactly (che/env/hazard.py:8-16; Appendix A), and cells outside the arena never burn.

Proposition 2 (Bond percolation). Under Definition 3 with \(\iota = 0\) and a single ignition at the origin, the set of eventually-Burnt cells is equal in distribution to the open cluster of the origin in i.i.d. bond percolation on \((G, E)\) with edge-open probability \(\beta\).

Here \(E\) is the arena's set of von Neumann edges. The proof (Appendix A) is a short coupling, the correspondence between an epidemic with a constant infectious period and bond percolation described by Grassberger (1983)[9]. Its hypotheses hold for CHE's fire when Coupling A is off, since collapses otherwise add ignitions.

Smoke outlives flame. Per cell,

\[\rho' = e^{-\eta}\rho + \sigma_s \mathbf{1}[h' = \mathrm{Burning}],\]

with emission \(\sigma_s = 1.0\) and decay \(\eta = 0.5\), held fixed by convention and never calibratedDX-1. A cell emits \(\sigma_s\) once, in the step it burns, and its smoke then decays geometrically, with no spatial smoothing (che/env/hazard.py:67-79).

3.3 Agents, deaths and a hazard-blind reward

The agents kernel \(T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')\) acts in three deterministic stages that consume no random numbers (che/env/env.py:159-173). An alive agent on a cell that collapses in this step is disabled before it moves. Survivors take one of 5ENV-8 actions, stay or a von Neumann move clipped to the grid, and a move into a collapsed cell is cancelled. An agent whose new cell is Burning in \(h'\) is then disabled, so a cell igniting under a standing agent kills it; Burning cells can be entered, and avoidance must be learned. A disabled agent stays disabled, holds still, collects nothing and disappears from the others' observations.

Definition 2 (Ambient hazard). The hazard is ambient if and only if (i) and (ii) hold.

(i) Reward independence: the reward is a function of task variables and of the agents' alive transition only,

\[\begin{aligned} R(s,a,s') = {} & R_{\mathrm{task}}(\mathrm{task}(x), a, \mathrm{task}(x')) \\ & - d_p\,\lvert\{i : \alpha_i = 1,\ \alpha'_i = 0\}\rvert , \end{aligned}\]

where \(\mathrm{task}(x)\) denotes the task variables (in CHE, the food grid and the alive agents' positions) and \(d_p \ge 0\) is a fixed death penalty. No term of \(R\) references \(h\), \(\rho\) or \(c\), and the hazard appears in no auxiliary cost or constraint channel: no CMDP constraint, no Lagrangian penalty, no safety critic.

(ii) Non-adversarial: \(T_H\) is a fixed stochastic kernel; nothing in the environment optimizes, learns or best-responds.

Under clause (i), survival is learned through two hazard-blind routes: a dead agent collects no further task reward, and each death costs a fixed \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause, in every configuration reported here except the \(d_p\) ablation below (the code's default is 0ENV-1). Fire and collapse reach return only through the transition and observation kernels. A hazard-priced reward signals the hazard's state whether or not anything is harmed: JaxWildfire, for example, penalizes its agent at every step in proportion to the number of burning cells (Çakır et al., 2025)[13]. At \(d_p = 0\) only the first route would remain.

Listing 2's signature admits only the food and occupancy grids: no fire, smoke or structure argument can reach the task reward.

Listing 2. The task reward reads food and occupancy only.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/tasks.py:31-43
# che/env/tasks.py:31-43 (verbatim)
def task_step(food: jax.Array, occupancy: jax.Array) -> tuple[jax.Array, jax.Array]:
    """Collection: an occupied food cell yields +1 team reward and empties.

    Multiple agents on one food cell still collect a single item (+1).
    Returns (food', team_reward). Reads task variables and agent occupancy
    only — no hazard/smoke/structure arguments, by design (Def. 2).
    """
    chex.assert_type(food, jnp.uint8)
    chex.assert_equal_shape([food, occupancy])
    collected = (food == 1) & occupancy
    reward = collected.sum().astype(jnp.float32)
    food_new = jnp.where(collected, jnp.uint8(0), food).astype(jnp.uint8)
    return food_new, reward

One test of clause (i) asserts identical rewards, with and without the death penalty, for states that differ only in fire, smoke or structure; fire and structure differ only far from every agent, which pins the alive transition equal (che/tests/test_reward_independence.py:6-15). It shows only that the reward reads no hazard state directly, since the fire is meant to change return through deaths. The other kills a standing agent by fire and, separately, by collapse, with identical task state, and asserts a reward of \(-d_p\) both times (che/tests/test_lethality.py:143-162).

Clause (i) also fixes how §9's two co-primary outcomes enter learning: survival is a penalized channel, each death costing \(d_p\), and completion a rewarded one, each collected item earning +1ENV-9. §9.2 reads any asymmetry between them as one between a penalized channel and a rewarded one.

The one ablation of \(d_p\) is from the calibration phase, with this scope: the fire alone with no coupling on, 500 updatesENV-2, 3 seeds per cellENV-2, an observation encoding that predates the one used here, and means over the final training updates. Raising \(d_p\) from 0ENV-2 to 0.5ENV-1 moved High survival from 0.575 to 0.866ENV-2 and High completion from 0.765 to 0.821ENV-3, moved Medium survival from 0.931 to 0.951 with completion 0.750 at bothENV-4, and left Low tied, 0.991 against 0.992ENV-5. It was not re-run under the current observation or with the couplings.

Clause (ii) does not make the hazard independent of the agents: a weak cell with an agent on it is nineCA-8 times as likely to collapse in a step as an empty one, and a collapse can seed fire (§5.1).

3.4 Nested models: ablations are exact by construction

Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel, and setting it to zero recovers a nested special case of the same model.

stressor parameter lives in off (\(=0\)) recovers test (che/tests/)
Coupling A (§5) \(\kappa_A\) \(T_H\), through \(c' - c\) a fire blind to the structure test_nesting.py
Coupling B (§6) \(\kappa_B\) \(O^i\) the pre-masking observation, plus an all-ones visibility plane Listing 3
communication denial (§7) \(\delta\) \(T_K\) free, range-limited communication test_comms.py

The fire's \(\beta\) is a parameter of the same kind, positive in every configuration and set by severity (§4).

One engineering rule makes the nesting exact: every stochastic branch draws its uniforms unconditionally and compares them against a probability that may be zero. The draws for Coupling B's reveal, the links and the per-episode mixture use streams derived from the reset or step key by a fixed tag, so adding them left the existing streams untouched. Setting a stressor to zero therefore changes comparisons only, and the same keys reproduce the nested model's trajectory bit for bit.

Listing 3 runs the environment at \(\kappa_B = 0\) with and without masking, under the same keys, and asserts bitwise-identical fire, smoke, structure, food, positions, alive flags and rewards. The rest of the test (che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:143-156) steps both by hand and asserts identical content planes, an all-ones visibility plane and a zero masked share.

Listing 3. With \(\kappa_B = 0\), trajectories and rewards are bitwise those of the environment before masking.

hidden commentLISTING: che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:131-142
# che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:131-142 (verbatim)
def test_kappa_b_zero_bitwise_recovers_obs_v2():
    """Nesting (M4.1): obs v3 at kappa_B = 0 is the pre-masking env plus a
    constant all-ones visibility plane — state trajectories, rewards, and
    content planes bitwise identical to obs v2 under the same keys."""
    theta = dataclasses.replace(_HOT, kappa_B=0.0)
    cfg3 = EnvConfig(grid_size=L, n_agents=4, horizon=64, theta=theta)
    cfg2 = dataclasses.replace(cfg3, obs_version=2)
    a = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg3)
    b = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg2)
    _assert_bitwise_equal(
        a, b, ("hazard", "smoke", "structure", "food", "pos", "alive", "reward")
    )

With \(\kappa_A = 0\), collapse activity leaves the fire and smoke bitwise unchanged (che/tests/test_nesting.py:78-88); at \(\delta = 0\), the environment reproduces, on the CPU, a trajectory digest recorded before the communication channel existed (che/tests/test_comms.py:272-319). A structural test also asserts that the traced computation keeps its random-number primitives at parameter value zero, catching a gate such as if kappa_A > 0: that behavioural tests miss (che/tests/test_nesting.py:222-240).

3.5 The instance, training and metrics

Every trained policy and evaluation in this paper uses one instance: a 64 × 64 grid, 12 agents, a horizon of 256 steps, a 9 × 9 egocentric crop and 32 food itemsENV-6. Food sits on distinct cells from reset and does not respawn, and an alive agent on a food cell collects it for a team reward of +1ENV-9. The observation's 8 channelsENV-7 are sixENV-7 indicators (burning, burnt, food, weak, collapsed, alive occupancy), the raw smoke density and Coupling B's visibility plane, which marks the revealed cells. An own-state vector gives the agent's row and column divided by the grid side, its alive flag and \(t\) over the horizonENV-7.

All agents share one actor-critic network over the crop, the own-state vector and the aggregate of delivered messages. It has two convolutions with 16 and 32 channels, a dense layer of 128 units, and separate actor and critic heads with a 64-unit layer eachENV-10. A message head receives no gradient; what it emits is a frozen-at-initialization projection of trained trunk features (§7).

Training is the independent PPO of de Witt et al. (2020)[11], in the PureJaxRL style of Lu et al. (2022)[38], on the environment conventions of JaxMARL (Rutherford et al., 2024)[16]. Each seed trains one shared-parameter policyENV-11, whose update takes each agent's transitions as separate samples under the team reward. Each update collects 128 steps from 256 parallel environmentsDX-2, 32,768 environment stepsDX-3, each advancing all 12 agentsENV-6. ISO, ISO-4 and JOINT (§9) each train for T* = 1000 updatesDES-13, or 32,768,000 environment stepsDX-3, with the hyperparameters of Appendix B.

The co-primary outcomes of §9 are per-episode. Completion is \(1 - F/32\)ENV-6, where \(F\) is the number of food items left at the episode's last step; survival rate is the fraction of the 12 agentsENV-6 alive at the end. Episodes always run to the horizon, even when every agent has died (che/env/env.py:361; che/eval/harness.py:44-45). Deaths are also recorded by cause, as diagnostics.

Kit source of this part sections/03_environment.md

CHE is a grid world in which a team of agents forages while a fire burns; Figure 4 shows one step.

{% include figure.html path="assets/img/submission/fig01_schematic.png" class="img-fluid" alt="Five numbered boxes in a row, collapse, hazard, smoke, agents and comms, with a Coupling A arrow from collapse into the hazard box, a Coupling B arrow from smoke down to an observation box drawn from the post-step state, and comms denial entering the comms box with no hazard input." caption="Figure 4. One environment step of CHE." %}

<p class="caption">Collapse, fire, smoke, agents and communication are sampled in the order of Proposition 1. Coupling A (orange) carries c′ − c into the fire; Coupling B (blue) carries ρ′ into the observation, drawn from s′. The comms kernel takes δ and reads no hazard; T<sub>X</sub> reads the post-step h′ and c′, as in the code. Arrow colours are this figure's own. The reward is not drawn (Definition 2). <span class="figure-note">Status: schematic, no data.</span></p>

### 3.1 State, kernel and step order

**Definition 1 (Compound Hostile Environment).** A CHE is a Dec-POMDP $\mathcal{M}_\theta = \langle I, \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}, T_\theta, R, \{\Omega_i\}, O_\theta, \gamma \rangle$ with a finite set of agents $I$ on a grid $G$, action sets $\mathcal{A}_i$, observation sets $\Omega_i$, reward $R$ (Definition 2), observation kernel $O_\theta$ and discount $\gamma$. It is indexed by the *environment configuration* $\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)$: the fire's per-neighbour ignition probability $\beta$, the strengths $\kappa_A$ and $\kappa_B$ of Couplings A and B, and the communication-denial level $\delta$. Its state space factors as

$$
\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{H} \times \mathcal{P} \times \mathcal{C} \times \mathcal{K} \times \{0, \dots, T\},
$$

where $x \in \mathcal{X}$ holds the agents' positions $x_i \in G$, their alive flags $\alpha_i \in \{0, 1\}$ and the task state; $h \in \mathcal{H} = \{\mathrm{Fuel}, \mathrm{Burning}, \mathrm{Burnt}\}^G$ is the fire; $\rho \in \mathcal{P} = \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^G$ is the smoke field; $c \in \mathcal{C} = \{\mathrm{intact}, \mathrm{collapsed}\}^G$ is the structure; $k \in \mathcal{K}$ is the realized communication graph on $I$; and the last factor is the step index $t$, with horizon $T$. The transition kernel factorizes as

$$
\begin{aligned}
T_\theta(s' \mid s, a) = {} & \underbrace{T_C(c' \mid c, x)}_{\mathrm{collapse}} \; \underbrace{T_H^{\beta,\kappa_A}(h' \mid h, c, c')}_{\mathrm{fire;\ Coupling\ A}} \; \underbrace{T_\rho(\rho' \mid \rho, h')}_{\mathrm{smoke}} \\
& \times \underbrace{T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')}_{\mathrm{agents}} \; \underbrace{T_K^{\delta}(k' \mid x')}_{\mathrm{comms}},
\end{aligned}
$$

and agent $i$'s observation is drawn from the post-step state, $o'_i \sim O^i_{\kappa_B}(\cdot \mid x', h', \rho', c', k')$. The dependence of $T_H$ on the collapse increment $c' - c$ is Coupling A (§5); the dependence of $O^i_{\kappa_B}$ on the fire, through the smoke field $\rho'$ it produces, is Coupling B (§6). A training mixture (§9.1) draws $\theta$ once per episode, at reset. The slopes $\gamma_p$ and $\gamma_n$ (§9.2, Appendix H) are unrelated to this discount.

**Proposition 1 (Well-posedness and Markov closure).** For every $\theta$, the process $s_t = (x_t, h_t, \rho_t, c_t, k_t, t)$ under any joint policy is a Markov chain, and $\mathcal{M}_\theta$ is a well-defined Dec-POMDP: finite-horizon optimal joint policies exist, and the standard value definitions apply.

The proof (Appendix A) composes the five kernels in order. Because $\rho$ is real-valued the state space is not finite, but the states and observations reachable within the horizon form finite sets, and restricted to them $\mathcal{M}_\theta$ is a finite Dec-POMDP.

**Remark 1 (Hardness).** For two or more agents, the finite-horizon Dec-POMDP decision problem is NEXP-hard, and NEXP-complete when the horizon is below the number of states (Bernstein et al., 2002)<d-cite key="bernstein2002"></d-cite>. This bounds the worst case over the class and says nothing about a particular instance; it licenses the expectation that no general exact planner will scale, which is why we learn policies rather than plan.

Listing 1, the body of `step`, follows the factorization; the link graph is drawn on its own stream with no hazard argument, and the reward line reads only the task reward and the two death counts. In the listing, `tl` is the episode's $\theta$, and no configuration here uses the early `frozen` control branch. The observation is then drawn from the new state on its own stream (`che/env/env.py:394-396`).

**Listing 1.** One environment step in the order of Proposition 1.

<!-- LISTING: che/env/env.py:298-358 -->
```python
# che/env/env.py:298-358 (verbatim)
    k_struct, k_seed, k_fire = jax.random.split(key, 3)

    # 1. c' ~ T_C(c, x): reads *pre-step* occupancy.
    occ_pre = occupancy_grid(state.agent_pos, state.agent_alive, cfg.grid_size)
    structure_new = structure_step(
        k_struct,
        state.structure,
        state.weak,
        occ_pre.astype(jnp.float32),
        lambda_0=th.lambda_0,
        lambda_load=th.lambda_load,
    )
    collapse_increment = (structure_new == COLLAPSED) & (state.structure == INTACT)

    # 2. h' ~ T_H(h, c, c'): CA spread, then the Coupling A impulse from the
    # collapse increment (seeded cells are Burning in h', spread next step).
    seed_mask = coupling_a_seed_mask(
        k_seed, collapse_increment, kappa_A=tl.kappa_A, r_seed=th.r_seed
    )
    hazard_ca = hazard_step(k_fire, state.hazard, beta=tl.beta, iota=th.iota)
    if cfg.hazard_mode == "frozen":
        # M1.3: h is frozen — the CA/seed draws above still happen and are
        # discarded, so dynamic<->frozen with the same key share every
        # other stream bitwise (invariant #3). Frozen Burning cells stay
        # Burning: lethal and smoke-emitting, but never spread or burn out.
        hazard_new = state.hazard
        seeded_ignitions = jnp.zeros_like(seed_mask)
    else:
        hazard_new = seed_ignitions(hazard_ca, seed_mask)
        seeded_ignitions = hazard_ca != hazard_new  # Fuel cells Coupling A lit

    # 3. rho' from h' (Def. 6; smoke outlives flame).
    smoke_new = smoke_step(state.smoke, hazard_new, sigma_s=th.sigma_s, eta=th.eta)

    # 4. x' ~ T_X + task dynamics. M1.1: lethality/blocking against h'/c'.
    # DECISION: an agent disabled this step does not collect food this step
    # (it died before/on arrival) — occupancy filters on the post-death
    # alive vector, keeping "dead agents never collect" exact.
    pos_new, alive_new, deaths_fire, deaths_collapse, blocked_moves = agent_step(
        state.agent_pos,
        state.agent_alive,
        actions,
        hazard_new,
        structure_new,
        collapse_increment,
        cfg.grid_size,
    )
    occ_post = occupancy_grid(pos_new, alive_new, cfg.grid_size)
    food_new, task_reward = task_step(state.food, occ_post)
    # Def.-2-compliant death penalty: reads only the alpha transition (an X
    # variable) — never hazard/smoke/structure directly.
    reward = task_reward - th.death_penalty * (deaths_fire + deaths_collapse).astype(
        jnp.float32
    )

    # 5. k' ~ T_K(x'): comms channel (Def. 7, M5.0). Reads post-step
    # positions/aliveness only — never h', rho' or c' — and draws from its
    # own fold_in stream, so delta cannot perturb any kernel above.
    links, links_in_range = _comms_obs(
        jax.random.fold_in(key, _COMMS_STREAM), pos_new, alive_new, cfg, tl.delta
    )
```

### 3.2 The fire and its smoke

**Definition 3 (Fire kernel).** Each cell is Fuel, Burning or Burnt. At each step, independently for each ordered pair of a Burning cell $g$ and a Fuel von Neumann neighbour $g' \in N(g)$, the cell $g'$ ignites with probability $\beta$. A Burning cell becomes Burnt after exactly one step, and Burnt is absorbing. Fuel cells may also ignite spontaneously at rate $\iota \ge 0$ per step; CHE uses $\iota = 0$<!-- N:DX-1 -->.

Every episode starts from an all-Fuel grid with one<!-- N:V3-1 --> Burning cell at a uniformly random position (`che/env/env.py:241-243`). One uniform per cell and incoming direction realizes the per-edge Bernoulli variables of Proposition 2's coupling exactly (`che/env/hazard.py:8-16`; Appendix A), and cells outside the arena never burn.

**Proposition 2 (Bond percolation).** Under Definition 3 with $\iota = 0$ and a single ignition at the origin, the set of eventually-Burnt cells is equal in distribution to the open cluster of the origin in i.i.d. bond percolation on $(G, E)$ with edge-open probability $\beta$.

Here $E$ is the arena's set of von Neumann edges. The proof (Appendix A) is a short coupling, the correspondence between an epidemic with a constant infectious period and bond percolation described by Grassberger (1983)<d-cite key="grassberger1983"></d-cite>. Its hypotheses hold for CHE's fire when Coupling A is off, since collapses otherwise add ignitions.

Smoke outlives flame. Per cell,

$$
\rho' = e^{-\eta}\rho + \sigma_s \mathbf{1}[h' = \mathrm{Burning}],
$$

with emission $\sigma_s = 1.0$ and decay $\eta = 0.5$, held fixed by convention and never calibrated<!-- N:DX-1 -->. A cell emits $\sigma_s$ once, in the step it burns, and its smoke then decays geometrically, with no spatial smoothing (`che/env/hazard.py:67-79`).

### 3.3 Agents, deaths and a hazard-blind reward

The agents kernel $T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')$ acts in three deterministic stages that consume no random numbers (`che/env/env.py:159-173`). An alive agent on a cell that collapses in this step is disabled before it moves. Survivors take one of 5<!-- N:ENV-8 --> actions, stay or a von Neumann move clipped to the grid, and a move into a collapsed cell is cancelled. An agent whose new cell is Burning in $h'$ is then disabled, so a cell igniting under a standing agent kills it; Burning cells can be entered, and avoidance must be learned. A disabled agent stays disabled, holds still, collects nothing and disappears from the others' observations.

**Definition 2 (Ambient hazard).** The hazard is *ambient* if and only if (i) and (ii) hold.

(i) *Reward independence:* the reward is a function of task variables and of the agents' alive transition only,

$$
\begin{aligned}
R(s,a,s') = {} & R_{\mathrm{task}}(\mathrm{task}(x), a, \mathrm{task}(x')) \\
& - d_p\,\lvert\{i : \alpha_i = 1,\ \alpha'_i = 0\}\rvert ,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\mathrm{task}(x)$ denotes the task variables (in CHE, the food grid and the alive agents' positions) and $d_p \ge 0$ is a fixed death penalty. No term of $R$ references $h$, $\rho$ or $c$, and the hazard appears in no auxiliary cost or constraint channel: no CMDP constraint, no Lagrangian penalty, no safety critic.

(ii) *Non-adversarial:* $T_H$ is a fixed stochastic kernel; nothing in the environment optimizes, learns or best-responds.

Under clause (i), survival is learned through two hazard-blind routes: a dead agent collects no further task reward, and each death costs a fixed $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, whatever its cause, in every configuration reported here except the $d_p$ ablation below (the code's default is 0<!-- N:ENV-1 -->). Fire and collapse reach return only through the transition and observation kernels. A hazard-priced reward signals the hazard's state whether or not anything is harmed: JaxWildfire, for example, penalizes its agent at every step in proportion to the number of burning cells (Çakır et al., 2025)<d-cite key="cakir2025jaxwildfire"></d-cite>. At $d_p = 0$ only the first route would remain.

Listing 2's signature admits only the food and occupancy grids: no fire, smoke or structure argument can reach the task reward.

**Listing 2.** The task reward reads food and occupancy only.

<!-- LISTING: che/env/tasks.py:31-43 -->
```python
# che/env/tasks.py:31-43 (verbatim)
def task_step(food: jax.Array, occupancy: jax.Array) -> tuple[jax.Array, jax.Array]:
    """Collection: an occupied food cell yields +1 team reward and empties.

    Multiple agents on one food cell still collect a single item (+1).
    Returns (food', team_reward). Reads task variables and agent occupancy
    only — no hazard/smoke/structure arguments, by design (Def. 2).
    """
    chex.assert_type(food, jnp.uint8)
    chex.assert_equal_shape([food, occupancy])
    collected = (food == 1) & occupancy
    reward = collected.sum().astype(jnp.float32)
    food_new = jnp.where(collected, jnp.uint8(0), food).astype(jnp.uint8)
    return food_new, reward
```

One test of clause (i) asserts identical rewards, with and without the death penalty, for states that differ only in fire, smoke or structure; fire and structure differ only far from every agent, which pins the alive transition equal (`che/tests/test_reward_independence.py:6-15`). It shows only that the reward reads no hazard state directly, since the fire is meant to change return through deaths. The other kills a standing agent by fire and, separately, by collapse, with identical task state, and asserts a reward of $-d_p$ both times (`che/tests/test_lethality.py:143-162`).

Clause (i) also fixes how §9's two co-primary outcomes enter learning: survival is a penalized channel, each death costing $d_p$, and completion a rewarded one, each collected item earning +1<!-- N:ENV-9 -->. §9.2 reads any asymmetry between them as one between a penalized channel and a rewarded one.

The one ablation of $d_p$ is from the calibration phase, with this scope: the fire alone with no coupling on, 500 updates<!-- N:ENV-2 -->, 3 seeds per cell<!-- N:ENV-2 -->, an observation encoding that predates the one used here, and means over the final training updates. Raising $d_p$ from 0<!-- N:ENV-2 --> to 0.5<!-- N:ENV-1 --> moved High survival from 0.575 to 0.866<!-- N:ENV-2 --> and High completion from 0.765 to 0.821<!-- N:ENV-3 -->, moved Medium survival from 0.931 to 0.951 with completion 0.750 at both<!-- N:ENV-4 -->, and left Low tied, 0.991 against 0.992<!-- N:ENV-5 -->. It was not re-run under the current observation or with the couplings.

Clause (ii) does not make the hazard independent of the agents: a weak cell with an agent on it is nine<!-- N:CA-8 --> times as likely to collapse in a step as an empty one, and a collapse can seed fire (§5.1).

### 3.4 Nested models: ablations are exact by construction

Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel, and setting it to zero recovers a nested special case of the same model.

| stressor | parameter | lives in | off ($=0$) recovers | test (`che/tests/`) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Coupling A (§5) | $\kappa_A$ | $T_H$, through $c' - c$ | a fire blind to the structure | `test_nesting.py` |
| Coupling B (§6) | $\kappa_B$ | $O^i$ | the pre-masking observation, plus an all-ones visibility plane | Listing 3 |
| communication denial (§7) | $\delta$ | $T_K$ | free, range-limited communication | `test_comms.py` |

The fire's $\beta$ is a parameter of the same kind, positive in every configuration and set by severity (§4).

One engineering rule makes the nesting exact: every stochastic branch draws its uniforms unconditionally and compares them against a probability that may be zero. The draws for Coupling B's reveal, the links and the per-episode mixture use streams derived from the reset or step key by a fixed tag, so adding them left the existing streams untouched. Setting a stressor to zero therefore changes comparisons only, and the same keys reproduce the nested model's trajectory bit for bit.

Listing 3 runs the environment at $\kappa_B = 0$ with and without masking, under the same keys, and asserts bitwise-identical fire, smoke, structure, food, positions, alive flags and rewards. The rest of the test (`che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:143-156`) steps both by hand and asserts identical content planes, an all-ones visibility plane and a zero masked share.

**Listing 3.** With $\kappa_B = 0$, trajectories and rewards are bitwise those of the environment before masking.

<!-- LISTING: che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:131-142 -->
```python
# che/tests/test_coupling_b.py:131-142 (verbatim)
def test_kappa_b_zero_bitwise_recovers_obs_v2():
    """Nesting (M4.1): obs v3 at kappa_B = 0 is the pre-masking env plus a
    constant all-ones visibility plane — state trajectories, rewards, and
    content planes bitwise identical to obs v2 under the same keys."""
    theta = dataclasses.replace(_HOT, kappa_B=0.0)
    cfg3 = EnvConfig(grid_size=L, n_agents=4, horizon=64, theta=theta)
    cfg2 = dataclasses.replace(cfg3, obs_version=2)
    a = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg3)
    b = _traj(theta, cfg=cfg2)
    _assert_bitwise_equal(
        a, b, ("hazard", "smoke", "structure", "food", "pos", "alive", "reward")
    )
```

With $\kappa_A = 0$, collapse activity leaves the fire and smoke bitwise unchanged (`che/tests/test_nesting.py:78-88`); at $\delta = 0$, the environment reproduces, on the CPU, a trajectory digest recorded before the communication channel existed (`che/tests/test_comms.py:272-319`). A structural test also asserts that the traced computation keeps its random-number primitives at parameter value zero, catching a gate such as `if kappa_A > 0:` that behavioural tests miss (`che/tests/test_nesting.py:222-240`).

### 3.5 The instance, training and metrics

Every trained policy and evaluation in this paper uses one instance: a 64 × 64 grid, 12 agents, a horizon of 256 steps, a 9 × 9 egocentric crop and 32 food items<!-- N:ENV-6 -->. Food sits on distinct cells from reset and does not respawn, and an alive agent on a food cell collects it for a team reward of +1<!-- N:ENV-9 -->. The observation's 8 channels<!-- N:ENV-7 --> are six<!-- N:ENV-7 --> indicators (burning, burnt, food, weak, collapsed, alive occupancy), the raw smoke density and Coupling B's visibility plane, which marks the revealed cells. An own-state vector gives the agent's row and column divided by the grid side, its alive flag and $t$ over the horizon<!-- N:ENV-7 -->.

All agents share one actor-critic network over the crop, the own-state vector and the aggregate of delivered messages. It has two convolutions with 16 and 32 channels, a dense layer of 128 units, and separate actor and critic heads with a 64-unit layer each<!-- N:ENV-10 -->. A message head receives no gradient; what it emits is a frozen-at-initialization projection of trained trunk features (§7).

Training is the independent PPO of de Witt et al. (2020)<d-cite key="dewitt2020ippo"></d-cite>, in the PureJaxRL style of Lu et al. (2022)<d-cite key="lu2022dpo"></d-cite>, on the environment conventions of JaxMARL (Rutherford et al., 2024)<d-cite key="rutherford2024jaxmarl"></d-cite>. Each seed trains one shared-parameter policy<!-- N:ENV-11 -->, whose update takes each agent's transitions as separate samples under the team reward. Each update collects 128 steps from 256 parallel environments<!-- N:DX-2 -->, 32,768 environment steps<!-- N:DX-3 -->, each advancing all 12 agents<!-- N:ENV-6 -->. ISO, ISO-4 and JOINT (§9) each train for T\* = 1000 updates<!-- N:DES-13 -->, or 32,768,000 environment steps<!-- N:DX-3 -->, with the hyperparameters of Appendix B.

The co-primary outcomes of §9 are per-episode. *Completion* is $1 - F/32$<!-- N:ENV-6 -->, where $F$ is the number of food items left at the episode's last step; *survival rate* is the fraction of the 12 agents<!-- N:ENV-6 --> alive at the end. Episodes always run to the horizon, even when every agent has died (`che/env/env.py:361`; `che/eval/harness.py:44-45`). Deaths are also recorded by cause, as diagnostics.
