Severity in CHE is placed by a measured dynamical phase of the fire: the threeCAL-3 levels sit below, near and above the implemented kernel's measured transition (Figure 5).

4.1 Phase structure of the idealized kernel

Corollary 1 (Phase structure). On \(\mathbb{Z}^2\) with the von Neumann neighbourhood, the fire of Definition 3 with \(\iota = 0\) and a single ignition at the origin inherits the phase structure of bond percolation (Proposition 2, whose coupling holds on any graph):

  1. Its critical point is \(\beta_c = 1/2\) exactly, the critical probability of bond percolation on the square lattice (Kesten, 1980)[10].
  2. For \(\beta > \beta_c\) the fire spreads indefinitely with positive probability. On that event the cells ignited by step \(n\) are those within \(n\) open edges of the origin. Scaled by \(1/n\), they converge almost surely in Hausdorff distance to a deterministic convex compact set with non-empty interior, invariant under permutations and reflections of the coordinates (Garet & Marchand, 2004; their Corollary 5.4)[39]. The growth is therefore linear in \(n\), at a speed given in each direction by the extent of that set.

Both items concern the idealized kernel (von Neumann neighbourhood, constant burn time, homogeneous fuel, no wind) on the infinite lattice. CHE's fire is that kernel on a finite arena, with ignitions added by Coupling A (§5.1), so the levels are placed against a measured finite-size threshold and, at High, a measured front speed (§4.3).

4.2 Severity as phase

Definition 4 (Severity regimes). Fix the arena side \(L\). Severity levels are defined by the position of the implemented kernel relative to its measured critical point \(\hat\beta_c\), through the correlation length \(\xi(\beta)\), the typical extent of a fire cluster:

  • Low, subcritical, \(\xi(\beta) \ll L\): fire clusters are local and short-lived, and survival is a local-avoidance problem.
  • Medium, near-critical, \(\xi(\beta) \sim L\): cluster sizes are scale-free up to the arena's size, fluctuations are maximal, and a single ignition can, with non-negligible probability, cascade to arena scale; survival is a global-anticipation problem.
  • High, supercritical: fire fronts move at a speed comparable to the agents', and survival is a race.

The correlation length motivates the levels; operationally, each level is the observable band it was locked to, that is, fixed on 2026-07-19DX-401, before the main experiment (§4.3). As qualitatively different phases of one kernel, the levels make training at Low and High and evaluating at Medium (§9.1) a test of transfer to a phase neither arm trained in, though in the scalar \(\beta\) it remains an interpolation (§10). Definition 4 also carried an expectation, on record before its test (§4.4), that per-episode outcome variance would peak at Medium.

4.3 Measuring the threshold and locking the levels

The calibration ran the fire alone on an all-Fuel grid at \(L \in \{32, 48, 64\}\)DX-7, with 512 seeds per \((L, \beta)\)DX-7. A span sweep over 35DX-7 values of \(\beta\) (0.05 to 0.95 coarsely, 0.40 to 0.60 in steps of 0.01DX-7), each from one centre ignition for \(T_{\max} = 4L\) stepsDX-7, measured \(P_{\mathrm{span}}\), the burnt fraction and the front speed. A crossing sweep ignited the full left column and recorded the left–right crossing probability \(R_L(\beta)\).

The severity lock fixed \(\hat\beta_c = 0.500 \pm 0.005\)CAL-1, from logistic fits to \(R_L\) with centres 0.4985, 0.5006 and 0.4999 at \(L = 32, 48, 64\)CAL-1, and a self-duality check \(R_L(0.500) = 0.488, 0.502, 0.494\)CAL-2. The lock record states that they were recomputed independently from the committed raw data; no committed script or artifact produces the logistic centres. Among the committed estimators, pairwise crossings of \(R_L\) average 0.504CAL-1; the family spans [0.480, 0.513]DX-204, with consensus mean 0.499CAL-1. Their range brackets Kesten's ½. That agreement validates the implementation; it is not a finding about fire, since Proposition 2 and Corollary 1 predict it for this kernel.

Each level was then locked to an observable band, with its measured values (\(L = 64\)DX-7, 512 seedsDX-7, no agents):

level \(\beta\) phase \(P_{\mathrm{span}}\) burnt fraction band the level was locked to
Low 0.43CAL-3 subcritical 0.021CAL-3 1.9 %CAL-3 \(P_{\mathrm{span}} < 0.05\) and burnt fraction in [1, 5] %DX-201
Medium 0.49CAL-3 near-critical 0.547CAL-3 19.8 %CAL-3 \(P_{\mathrm{span}}\) in [0.3, 0.7]DX-201
High 0.70CAL-3 supercritical 0.998CAL-3 98.3 %CAL-3 front speed in [0.5, 1.0] cells per stepDX-201, measured 0.83DX-6

At High the front moves at 83 %DX-6 of the agents' speed of one cell per stepDX-6; escape is possible in principle. Medium sits between the finite-size pseudo-critical point \(\beta_c(64) \approx 0.486\)CAL-4, where \(P_{\mathrm{span}} = 1/2\) at \(L = 64\)CAL-4, and \(\hat\beta_c\). It is the severity of the held-out evaluation configuration of §9, \(\theta^* = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\) = (0.49CAL-3, 0.06CA-1, 1.0CB-1, 1.0COM-6); the compositional test trains at Low and High only.

4.4 Re-testing the Medium prediction

The calibration-phase probe at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1 (the fire alone, 500 updatesDX-203, three seeds per severityDX-203) had saved no checkpoints. The re-test at fixed policy re-ran it from the same configurations and seeds (equivalent, not bitwise identical) and evaluated each final checkpoint on 512 episodesDX-203. For survival the prediction is refuted: per-episode variance at fixed policy is monotone in severity, 0.00091, 0.00709 and 0.00885 at Low, Medium and HighCAL-5. For completion it is not confirmed: Medium and Low tie within bootstrap intervals, 0.01245 against 0.01235DX-8, both above High. The environment-level mechanism is confirmed: with no agents, the burnt fraction's variance peaks near \(\beta \approx 0.53\)DX-8, just above Medium, and ranks Medium above High above Low at the locked levels.

The re-test report offers an account it did not test: outcome variance is roughly the environment's variance, which peaks near criticality, times the policy's sensitivity to it, which grows with severity. The refutation is scoped to survival, for these fire-only policies at this budget; §10 states what siting the held-out test at Medium costs.

### 4.1 Phase structure of the idealized kernel

**Corollary 1 (Phase structure).** On $\mathbb{Z}^2$ with the von Neumann neighbourhood, the fire of Definition 3 with $\iota = 0$ and a single ignition at the origin inherits the phase structure of bond percolation (Proposition 2, whose coupling holds on any graph):

1. Its critical point is $\beta_c = 1/2$ exactly, the critical probability of bond percolation on the square lattice (Kesten, 1980)<d-cite key="kesten1980"></d-cite>.
2. For $\beta > \beta_c$ the fire spreads indefinitely with positive probability. On that event the cells ignited by step $n$ are those within $n$ open edges of the origin. Scaled by $1/n$, they converge almost surely in Hausdorff distance to a deterministic convex compact set with non-empty interior, invariant under permutations and reflections of the coordinates (Garet & Marchand, 2004; their Corollary 5.4)<d-cite key="garet2004chemical"></d-cite>. The growth is therefore linear in $n$, at a speed given in each direction by the extent of that set.

Both items concern the idealized kernel (von Neumann neighbourhood, constant burn time, homogeneous fuel, no wind) on the infinite lattice. CHE's fire is that kernel on a finite arena, with ignitions added by Coupling A (§5.1), so the levels are placed against a measured finite-size threshold and, at High, a measured front speed (§4.3).

### 4.2 Severity as phase

**Definition 4 (Severity regimes).** Fix the arena side $L$. Severity levels are defined by the position of the implemented kernel relative to its measured critical point $\hat\beta_c$, through the correlation length $\xi(\beta)$, the typical extent of a fire cluster:

- *Low*, subcritical, $\xi(\beta) \ll L$: fire clusters are local and short-lived, and survival is a local-avoidance problem.
- *Medium*, near-critical, $\xi(\beta) \sim L$: cluster sizes are scale-free up to the arena's size, fluctuations are maximal, and a single ignition can, with non-negligible probability, cascade to arena scale; survival is a global-anticipation problem.
- *High*, supercritical: fire fronts move at a speed comparable to the agents', and survival is a race.

The correlation length motivates the levels; operationally, each level is the observable band it was locked to, that is, fixed on 2026-07-19<!-- N:DX-401 -->, before the main experiment (§4.3). Definition 4 also carried an expectation, on record before its test (§4.4), that per-episode outcome variance would peak at Medium.

The refutation is scoped to survival, for these fire-only policies at this budget; §10 states what siting the held-out test at Medium costs.

5 Coupling A: collapse seeds the fire

Coupling A lets structural collapse feed the fire. Figure 1's event track marks one random-policy episode's collapses and the ignitions they seeded (press ] in Figure 1 to step between them); Figure 6 counts them under trained policies.

5.1 Definition and implementation

Definition 5 (Coupling A, as implemented). Let \(w(g) \in \{0,1\}\) mark weak terrain, a spatially clustered fraction \(f_{\mathrm{weak}}\) of the cells drawn once per episode, and let \(o_t(g) \in \{0,1\}\) indicate that at least one alive agent stands on cell \(g\) before the step. An intact cell collapses at step \(t\) with probability

\[\lambda_t(g) = \big(\lambda_0 + \lambda_{\mathrm{load}}\,o_t(g)\big)\,w(g),\]

so a cell that is not weak never collapses, and collapse is absorbing. Coupling A then ignites, with probability \(\kappa_A\), each cell that is still Fuel after this step's spread and lies within Chebyshev distance \(r_A\) of a cell that collapsed in this step. The fire kernel thus reads only the collapse increment \(c' - c\), and a seeded cell is Burning in \(h'\). Setting \(\kappa_A = 0\) recovers the uncoupled fire kernel \(T_H^{\beta,0}\), so the ablation "no Coupling A" is a nested model of the full system.

The theory states the impulse per collapse event; the implementation draws one uniform per cell, and the two rules coincide wherever seeding neighbourhoods do not overlap. The values, locked on 2026-07-21DX-401, are \((f_{\mathrm{weak}}, \lambda_0, \lambda_{\mathrm{load}}, \kappa_A, r_A) = (0.15,\ 5\times 10^{-5},\ 4\times 10^{-4},\ 0.06,\ 1)\)CA-1, so the seeding neighbourhood is a 3 × 3DX-37 block.

Agents influence collapse, weakly and indirectly. Occupancy enters as a yes-or-no load: a weak cell with any agent on it collapses nine timesCA-8 as often as an empty one. Without load, the locked parameters give about 7.81CA-9 collapses per episode. Load adds 0.4 to 1.4 %CA-9 to that count under trained policies (the Coupling-A calibration grid of §5.3) and 7.4 to 8.3 %CA-9 under random ones, which is 6.9 to 7.6 %CA-9 of all collapses. Agents cannot ignite a cell, but where they stand changes collapse rates, and a collapse can seed fire.

Listing 4. Coupling A's seeding mask, with its uniforms drawn whatever \(\kappa_A\) is.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/structure.py:83-102
# che/env/structure.py:83-102 (verbatim)
def coupling_a_seed_mask(
    key: jax.Array,
    collapse_increment: jax.Array,
    *,
    kappa_A: float,
    r_seed: int,
) -> jax.Array:
    """Coupling A (Def. 5): each cell in the seeding neighborhood N_A of a
    new collapse (Chebyshev radius r_seed) ignites independently w.p.
    kappa_A. `collapse_increment` is the boolean mask of c' - c, i.e. cells
    that collapsed *this step* — T_H reads the increment, not the stock.
    Returns the candidate ignition mask; `hazard.seed_ignitions` applies it
    to Fuel cells only.
    """
    chex.assert_rank(collapse_increment, 2)
    chex.assert_type(collapse_increment, jnp.bool_)
    # Invariant #3: uniforms drawn even when kappa_A == 0.
    u = jax.random.uniform(key, collapse_increment.shape)
    near_new_collapse = dilate(collapse_increment, r_seed)
    return near_new_collapse & (u < kappa_A)

The uniforms u are drawn before anything reads \(\kappa_A\), so \(\kappa_A = 0\) is the nested model bit for bit under the rule of §3.4. The mask then goes to hazard.seed_ignitions, which lights only cells still Fuel; this is where the fuel limitation of §5.3 enters.

5.2 The handshake with Proposition 3

Proposition 3 (Collapse events dominate hazard exposure). Work in the subcritical regime \(\beta < \beta_c\) with \(\iota = 0\), and suppose collapse events arrive at rate \(\lambda_A\) per step, each seeding one ignition at a uniformly located Fuel cell, with seeded clusters pairwise disjoint (the sparse regime, \(\lambda_A T \chi(\beta) \ll \lvert G \rvert\), the number of cells). Then the expected burnt area \(B_T\) by time \(T\) satisfies

\[\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}[B_T] &= \lambda_A T\,\chi(\beta)\,\big(1 + o(1)\big), \\ \text{and in general} \quad \mathbb{E}[B_T] &\le \lambda_A T\,\chi(\beta), \end{aligned}\]

where \(\chi(\beta)\) is the mean percolation cluster size. Consequently the marginal hazard exposure per unit of structural instability is \(\partial\,\mathbb{E}[B_T]/\partial\lambda_A = T\chi(\beta)\).

The proof (Appendix A) grows each seed into a bond-percolation cluster by Proposition 2 and sums expectations; overlaps only remove double counting. With no primary ignition, as in both runs below, collapse is then the fire's only birth channel. In a finite protocol the equality is approached only with an unconditional cluster-mass reference (a spanning-conditioned one understates the mass), matched seed locations and ages, and sparse seeding; boundary clipping, seed age and overlapping seeds each lower the measured slope (Appendix A).

We tested the proposition in twoDX-304 runs, neither at the locked parameters, both without agents or primary ignition at the subcritical Low level (\(\beta = 0.43\), \(\iota = 0\)DX-304). The first, a dense sweep on a 64 × 64CA-2 grid (\(\kappa_A = 0.02\), \(f_{\mathrm{weak}} = 0.4\)CA-2), fits burnt area against realized seeds through the origin: \(R^2 = 0.998\)CA-2 over a 14×CA-2 range of the expected seed count. The second, a slower test in the suite on a 32 × 32CA-3 grid (\(\kappa_A = 0.003\)CA-3), divides its slope by a protocol-matched reference computed inside the test: 0.992CA-3, inside the band [0.90, 1.02]CA-3, with \(R^2 = 0.9995\)CA-3.

5.3 Under trained policies the coupling limits itself

In the Coupling-B calibration grid (500 updatesDX-40 at each severity, \(\kappa_B\) off and on, Coupling A on), the \(\kappa_B = 1.0\)CB-1 arm records 4.05, 3.41 and 0.84CA-4 collapse-seeded ignitions per episode at Low, Medium and High, a 4.83-foldCA-4 fall (from unrounded counts). The Coupling-A calibration grid (\(\kappa_A\) off and on, with other policies, trained on the observation before masking) replicates it: 4.08, 3.42 and 0.83CA-5, a 4.92-foldCA-5 fall.

Collapse never reads \(\beta\); what changes with severity is where the seeds land. At High the fire burns 98.3 %CAL-3 of the arena in calibration, and the Fuel-only rule discards most seeding attempts. This concerns ignitions only: the Coupling-A grid's survival contrasts, on two seeds per armCA-7 and with no floor, cannot locate the coupling's largest effect on outcomes.

This fuel limitation was on record before any trained policy was measured. At the Coupling-A lock, the random-policy calibration put seeded ignitions at High about 5.7 timesDX-9 below Low at every \(\kappa_A\) tried, and the lock recorded it as the fuel-exhaustion self-limitation of the ignition channel. A later prediction was written after the trained results existed and before its own analysis. It held that Coupling A is fuel-limited at High, that the co-active count of §6.5 need not be monotone in severity, and that Medium may be its peak. The 4.83-foldCA-4 fall confirms the first, which earlier reports already answered. The count is monotone non-increasing and Low and Medium are indistinguishable, so the second is not supported and the third is refuted.

Across all nineCA-6 measured cells (two arms of the Coupling-B grid and one of the Coupling-A grid, at threeCAL-3 severities), the share of seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop stays in [0.164, 0.203]CA-6.

At Low the coupling moves survival in the Coupling-A grid off its ceiling, from 0.979 to 0.961CA-7, as fire deaths per episode rise from 0.062 to 0.229DX-10. Weak-cell occupancy is higher by 0.054CA-7 with the coupling on, which suggests, on two seeds per armCA-7, that the policies do not learn to avoid weak cells. No contrast in this section has a floor on its own artifact, since neither grid has identical reruns; any floor beside them is a reference scale (cross-artifact); see §8.6.

### 5.1 Definition and implementation

**Definition 5 (Coupling A, as implemented).** Let $w(g) \in \{0,1\}$ mark weak terrain, a spatially clustered fraction $f_{\mathrm{weak}}$ of the cells drawn once per episode, and let $o_t(g) \in \{0,1\}$ indicate that at least one alive agent stands on cell $g$ before the step. ```python

def coupling_a_seed_mask(
    key: jax.Array,
    collapse_increment: jax.Array,
    *,
    kappa_A: float,
    r_seed: int,
) -> jax.Array:
    """Coupling A (Def. 5): each cell in the seeding neighborhood N_A of a
    new collapse (Chebyshev radius r_seed) ignites independently w.p.
    kappa_A. `collapse_increment` is the boolean mask of c' - c, i.e. cells
    that collapsed *this step* — T_H reads the increment, not the stock.
    Returns the candidate ignition mask; `hazard.seed_ignitions` applies it
    to Fuel cells only.

    chex.assert_type(collapse_increment, jnp.bool_)

    u = jax.random.uniform(key, collapse_increment.shape)

### 5.2 The handshake with Proposition 3

**Proposition 3 (Collapse events dominate hazard exposure).** Work in the subcritical regime $\beta < \beta_c$ with $\iota = 0$, and suppose collapse events arrive at rate $\lambda_A$ per step, each seeding one ignition at a uniformly located Fuel cell, with seeded clusters pairwise disjoint (the sparse regime, $\lambda_A T \chi(\beta) \ll \lvert G \rvert$, the number of cells). $$
\begin{aligned}

\text{and in general} \quad \mathbb{E}[B_T] &\le \lambda_A T\,\chi(\beta),
\end{aligned}
$$

The proof (Appendix A) grows each seed into a bond-percolation cluster by Proposition 2 and sums expectations; overlaps only remove double counting. ### 5.3 Under trained policies the coupling limits itself

In the Coupling-B calibration grid (500 updates<!-- N:DX-40 --> at each severity, $\kappa_B$ off and on, Coupling A on), the $\kappa_B = 1.0$<!-- N:CB-1 --> arm records 4.05, 3.41 and 0.84<!-- N:CA-4 --> collapse-seeded ignitions per episode at Low, Medium and High, a 4.83-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> fall (from unrounded counts). The Coupling-A calibration grid ($\kappa_A$ off and on, with other policies, trained on the observation before masking) replicates it: 4.08, 3.42 and 0.83<!-- N:CA-5 -->, a 4.92-fold<!-- N:CA-5 --> fall.

Collapse never reads $\beta$; what changes with severity is where the seeds land. At High the fire burns 98.3 %<!-- N:CAL-3 --> of the arena in calibration, and the Fuel-only rule discards most seeding attempts. This concerns ignitions only: the Coupling-A grid's survival contrasts, on two seeds per arm<!-- N:CA-7 --> and with no floor, cannot locate the coupling's largest effect on outcomes.

This fuel limitation was on record before any trained policy was measured. At the Coupling-A lock, the random-policy calibration put seeded ignitions at High about 5.7 times<!-- N:DX-9 --> below Low at every $\kappa_A$ tried, and the lock recorded it as the fuel-exhaustion self-limitation of the ignition channel. A later prediction was written after the trained results existed and before its own analysis. It held that Coupling A is fuel-limited at High, that the co-active count of §6.5 need not be monotone in severity, and that Medium may be its peak. The 4.83-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> fall confirms the first, which earlier reports already answered. The count is monotone non-increasing and Low and Medium are indistinguishable, so the second is not supported and the third is refuted.

Across all nine<!-- N:CA-6 --> measured cells (two arms of the Coupling-B grid and one of the Coupling-A grid, at three<!-- N:CAL-3 --> severities), the share of seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop stays in [0.164, 0.203]<!-- N:CA-6 -->.

At Low the coupling moves survival in the Coupling-A grid off its ceiling, from 0.979 to 0.961<!-- N:CA-7 -->, as fire deaths per episode rise from 0.062 to 0.229<!-- N:DX-10 -->. Weak-cell occupancy is higher by 0.054<!-- N:CA-7 --> with the coupling on, which suggests, on two seeds per arm<!-- N:CA-7 -->, that the policies do not learn to avoid weak cells. No contrast in this section has a floor on its own artifact, since neither grid has identical reruns; any floor beside them is a reference scale (cross-artifact); see §8.6.

Coupling B makes what an agent perceives depend on the hazard's own state. Figure 1 opens on agent 7DX-33 at t = 128DX-33 and shows that agent's observation as produced, the true state of the same 9 × 9ENV-6 cells, and the probability that each cell was seen.

6.1 Definition and implementation

Definition 6 (Coupling B: Beer–Lambert observation, as implemented). Smoke is the per-cell state field \(\rho'\) of §3.2, updated after the fire with no spatial smoothing, so it outlives flame. For agent \(i\) at \(x_i\) and a cell \(y\) of its crop, let \(\bar\rho_i(y)\) be the mean of \(\rho'\) at the four points \(x_i + t_s(y - x_i)\), \(t_s = (s + \tfrac12)/4\) for \(s = 0, \dots, 3\), each read at the nearest cell (smoke outside the grid reads 0). The transmittance is

\[\tau_i(y) = \exp\!\big(-\kappa_B\,\lVert y - x_i \rVert_2\,\bar\rho_i(y)\big),\]

a midpoint-rule form of \(\exp(-\kappa_B \int_{x_i \to y} \rho\,d\ell)\) along the line of sight. Each crop cell is revealed independently with probability \(\tau_i(y)\): a revealed cell passes its sevenENV-7 content planes, an unrevealed one passes zeros, and an eighthENV-7 plane records the realized reveal, so the agent can tell "unseen" from "empty". The own cell has distance 0 and is always seen. Setting \(\kappa_B = 0\) gives \(\tau \equiv 1\) and recovers hazard-independent observation.

Because \(\rho'\) is hazard state, perception degrades where and when the fire is active.

Listing 5. Coupling B in the observation kernel: transmittance, the reveal draw and the visibility plane.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/observation.py:300-310
# che/env/observation.py:300-310 (verbatim)
        tau = transmittance(
            state.smoke, state.agent_pos, kappa_B=state.theta_live.kappa_B, k=k
        )
        reveal = jax.random.uniform(key, tau.shape) < tau  # P(seen) = tau
        grid = jnp.concatenate(
            [
                grid * reveal[..., None].astype(jnp.float32),
                reveal[..., None].astype(jnp.float32),
            ],
            axis=-1,
        )

The reveal line draws its uniforms whatever \(\kappa_B\) is, so at \(\kappa_B = 0\) the mask is all ones and the environment is bitwise the one before masking (§3.4, Listing 3). The draw has its own random stream, and \(\kappa_B\) is read per episode from the state, which is how a training mixture varies it.

6.2 What attenuation does to the value of adaptivity

Theorem 1 (Memorization gap, and its erosion by Coupling B). Consider the two-corridor environment \(E_{2C}(\kappa_B)\): from a start cell, a path of \(d\) steps leads to a branch \(b\), from which two disjoint corridors \(L\) and \(R\) of length \(\ell\) lead to the goal; the horizon is \(T = d + \ell\) (zero slack: a wrong commitment cannot be undone). At \(t = 0\) nature draws \(Z \in \{L, R\}\) uniformly; corridor \(Z\) burns at depth \(\ell_f \ge 1\) beyond \(b\), and an agent entering it is disabled. The reward is 1 for reaching the goal by \(T\), else 0. At each step \(t \le d\) the agent receives the signal \(Y_t = Z\) with probability \(e^{-\kappa_B (d - t + \ell_f)}\) (Beer–Lambert over the current distance to the fire, unit smoke density), else \(Y_t = \varnothing\); signals are independent across time and remembered. Let

\[q(\kappa_B) = 1 - \prod_{j=\ell_f}^{d+\ell_f} \big(1 - e^{-\kappa_B j}\big)\]

be the probability of being informed by the commitment point. Then (1) the optimal value is \(J^*(\kappa_B) = \tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B)\); (2) the policies optimal for the fixed-map variant (\(Z \equiv z_0\) constant) include the signal-blind policy "always take the corridor other than \(z_0\)", whose value on \(E_{2C}\) is \(\tfrac12\) for every \(\kappa_B\), so the memorization gap is \(J^*(\kappa_B) - J^{\mathrm{static}} = \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B) > 0\) for all finite \(\kappa_B\); and (3) \(q\) is continuous and strictly decreasing in \(\kappa_B\), with \(q(0^+) \to 1\) and \(q(\kappa_B) \to 0\) as \(\kappa_B \to \infty\), so under total perceptual denial the optimal adaptive policy is no better than the memorizing one.

In one toy (proof in Appendix A), a memorizing policy loses exactly half the probability of being informed, and the hazard's own attenuation shrinks that loss continuously to zero; the theorem says nothing about what a learner does in CHE.

The theorem also tests the code. The toy and the observation kernel share one transmittance function, and the toy's information probability agrees at every grid point across Monte Carlo, the closed-form product and the full observation pipeline. The measured optimal value matches \(\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q\) under a three-conditionCB-4 gate: largest \(\lvert z \rvert\) 2.11CB-4, \(\sum z^2 = 6.55\)CB-4 on 8CB-4 degrees of freedom (\(p = 0.586\)CB-4), and mean \(z\) −0.44CB-4. The memorizing policy stays flat at ½DX-14, \(J^*(0) = 1.0000\)DX-14, and \(J^* - \tfrac12 = 0.0000\)DX-14 at \(\kappa_B = 8\), where \(q = 0.0056\)DX-14.

6.3 The lock: three bands that did not intersect

The strength \(\kappa_B\) was to be locked where threeDX-301 bands set before calibration intersect. They were a masked share of the crop at Medium in [0.15, 0.45]DX-301, a probability in [0.4, 0.7]DX-301 that a Burning cell threeDX-301 cells away is revealed at Medium, and a toy information probability \(q\) in [0.3, 0.7]DX-301. They do not intersect. The masked-share band cannot be reached at Medium: the masked share can only approach the share of crop cells with smoke on their line of sight, 0.130DX-12 there under a random policy, below the band's lower edge of 0.150DX-12. The same \(\kappa_B\) attenuates the arena's extended smoke more strongly than the toy's single smoke source, which leaves the detection and toy bands disjoint by a factor of 1.35 under a random policy and 1.15 under probe policiesCB-2. On 2026-07-27DX-401, \(\kappa_B = 1.0\)CB-1 was locked on the detection band alone; the masked-share band was retired, not widened, and the toy band kept as a consistency check.

6.4 Survival cost at High severity

(a) Masked share of the crop in the coupled arm of the Coupling-B calibration grid (labelled m44 and M4.4), over all alive agent-steps and at danger moments. (b) The High survival contrasts between the arms: three replicatesDX-13, then the first estimate. The hatched band, labelled floor, is a reference scale (cross-artifact). The figure cannot show intervals, completion or Medium. Status: trained policies, 500 updatesDX-40 · static.

At High, threeDX-13 replicate contrasts of survival between \(\kappa_B = 1.0\)CB-1 and \(\kappa_B = 0\) are negative three times out of threeCB-5 (Figure 7(b); trained policies, 500 updatesDX-40, \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, Coupling A on). They are −6.87 and −10.66 pointsDX-13 for the grid's two seedsDX-40 and −4.70 points for a same-seed retrainDX-13, each one run per cellDX-13 and without an interval. The grid's first estimate, the mean of its two seedsCB-6, was −8.8 pointsCB-6. The High survival floor, sd 6.21 pointsCB-7, was measured later on four identical runsDX-40 of a separate artifact: a reference scale (cross-artifact). The first estimate is 1.41 timesCB-5 this floor and the replicates' mean about 1.19 timesCB-5 it. We report a consistent effect of modest size; the first analysis graded it "strong" against a two-seedDX-38 spread, a label retracted under the rule of §8.1. Completion is not resolved, since its contrast flipped sign on the same-seed retrainCB-8.

At Medium, the severity of the held-out test configuration (β = 0.49CAL-3), Coupling B's own survival effect in the same grid was −0.03 pointsCB-9. It was judged against a three-seedCB-9 spread, \(\sigma_{\mathrm{seed}} = 0.72\) pointsCB-9, with no measured floor; the Medium survival floors measured later, on twoMETH-1 GPUs, are 1.29 and 1.30 pointsMETH-1. Coupling B's measured survival cost is therefore at High, while §9's test runs at Medium (§10, item 1).

The masking concentrates on moments of danger, alive agent-steps with a Burning cell inside the agent's crop. In the \(\kappa_B = 1.0\)CB-1 arm the masked share of the crop at danger moments is 0.0809, 0.0560 and 0.2424DX-11 at Low, Medium and High, against 0.00068, 0.00249 and 0.01559DX-11 over all alive agent-steps: 118, 22 and 16 timesCB-10 more concentrated (ratios of unrounded shares; Figure 7(a)). Within trained policies at 500 updatesDX-40 the attenuation was not behaviourally suppressed: the coupled policies' masking ceiling, which does not depend on \(\kappa_B\), sat at or above its random-policy value at every severity.

6.5 The co-active counter, and what it can see

The environment logs, at every step, the number of collapse-seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop, within Chebyshev radius 4DX-37 of its post-step positionCO-4. It was meant to catch both couplings at once, but only Coupling A produces those ignitions and \(\kappa_B\) does not enter the count, so the counter is co-active only in all-on configurations. In a training mixture it reads the Coupling-A marginal alone, which is why the mediation analysis registered on it is void (§9.2).

Share of episodes with each number of co-active events in the coupled arm of the Coupling-B calibration grid (labelled m44), seeds pooled; both couplings are on in every cell. The figure cannot show a floor, since none exists for this counter, or a training mixture (§6.5). Status: descriptive; trained policies, 500 updatesDX-40; no floor · static.

In these all-on cells the counter is rare and bursty (Figure 8). The share of episodes with no co-active event is 0.563, 0.578 and 0.883CO-2 at Low, Medium and High, the mean at Medium is 0.64CO-1 events per episode, and the variance-to-mean ratio, 1.31 to 1.40CO-3, exceeds the Poisson value of 1. Low and Medium are indistinguishable and High is lower, because seeded ignitions fall while the near-agent share stays in its range (§5.3).

### 6.2 What attenuation does to the value of adaptivity

**Theorem 1 (Memorization gap, and its erosion by Coupling B).** Consider the two-corridor environment $E_{2C}(\kappa_B)$: from a start cell, a path of $d$ steps leads to a branch $b$, from which two disjoint corridors $L$ and $R$ of length $\ell$ lead to the goal; the horizon is $T = d + \ell$ (zero slack: a wrong commitment cannot be undone). At $t = 0$ nature draws $Z \in \{L, R\}$ uniformly; corridor $Z$ burns at depth $\ell_f \ge 1$ beyond $b$, and an agent entering it is disabled. The reward is 1 for reaching the goal by $T$, else 0. At each step $t \le d$ the agent receives the signal $Y_t = Z$ with probability $e^{-\kappa_B (d - t + \ell_f)}$ (Beer–Lambert over the current distance to the fire, unit smoke density), else $Y_t = \varnothing$; signals are independent across time and remembered. Let

$$
q(\kappa_B) = 1 - \prod_{j=\ell_f}^{d+\ell_f} \big(1 - e^{-\kappa_B j}\big)
$$

be the probability of being informed by the commitment point. Then (1) the optimal value is $J^*(\kappa_B) = \tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B)$; (2) the policies optimal for the fixed-map variant ($Z \equiv z_0$ constant) include the signal-blind policy "always take the corridor other than $z_0$", whose value on $E_{2C}$ is $\tfrac12$ for every $\kappa_B$, so the memorization gap is $J^*(\kappa_B) - J^{\mathrm{static}} = \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B) > 0$ for all finite $\kappa_B$; and (3) $q$ is continuous and strictly decreasing in $\kappa_B$, with $q(0^+) \to 1$ and $q(\kappa_B) \to 0$ as $\kappa_B \to \infty$, so under total perceptual denial the optimal adaptive policy is no better than the memorizing one.

In one toy (proof in Appendix A), a memorizing policy loses exactly half the probability of being informed, and the hazard's own attenuation shrinks that loss continuously to zero; the theorem says nothing about what a learner does in CHE.

The theorem also tests the code. The toy and the observation kernel share one transmittance function, and the toy's information probability agrees at every grid point across Monte Carlo, the closed-form product and the full observation pipeline. The measured optimal value matches $\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q$ under a three-condition<!-- N:CB-4 --> gate: largest $\lvert z \rvert$ 2.11<!-- N:CB-4 -->, $\sum z^2 = 6.55$<!-- N:CB-4 --> on 8<!-- N:CB-4 --> degrees of freedom ($p = 0.586$<!-- N:CB-4 -->), and mean $z$ −0.44<!-- N:CB-4 -->. The memorizing policy stays flat at ½<!-- N:DX-14 -->, $J^*(0) = 1.0000$<!-- N:DX-14 -->, and $J^* - \tfrac12 = 0.0000$<!-- N:DX-14 --> at $\kappa_B = 8$, where $q = 0.0056$<!-- N:DX-14 -->.

### 6.3 The lock: three bands that did not intersect

The strength $\kappa_B$ was to be locked where three<!-- N:DX-301 --> bands set before calibration intersect. They were a masked share of the crop at Medium in [0.15, 0.45]<!-- N:DX-301 -->, a probability in [0.4, 0.7]<!-- N:DX-301 --> that a Burning cell three<!-- N:DX-301 --> cells away is revealed at Medium, and a toy information probability $q$ in [0.3, 0.7]<!-- N:DX-301 -->. They do not intersect. The masked-share band cannot be reached at Medium: the masked share can only approach the share of crop cells with smoke on their line of sight, 0.130<!-- N:DX-12 --> there under a random policy, below the band's lower edge of 0.150<!-- N:DX-12 -->. The same $\kappa_B$ attenuates the arena's extended smoke more strongly than the toy's single smoke source, which leaves the detection and toy bands disjoint by a factor of 1.35 under a random policy and 1.15 under probe policies<!-- N:CB-2 -->. On 2026-07-27<!-- N:DX-401 -->, $\kappa_B = 1.0$<!-- N:CB-1 --> was locked on the detection band alone; the masked-share band was retired, not widened, and the toy band kept as a consistency check.

### 6.4 Survival cost at High severity

<p class="caption">(a) Masked share of the crop in the coupled arm of the Coupling-B calibration grid (labelled m44 and M4.4), over all alive agent-steps and at danger moments. (b) The High survival contrasts between the arms: three replicates<!-- N:DX-13 -->, then the first estimate. The hatched band, labelled floor, is a reference scale (cross-artifact). The figure cannot show intervals, completion or Medium. <span class="figure-note">Status: trained policies, 500 updates<!-- N:DX-40 --> · static.</span></p>

At High, three<!-- N:DX-13 --> replicate contrasts of survival between $\kappa_B = 1.0$<!-- N:CB-1 --> and $\kappa_B = 0$ are negative three times out of three<!-- N:CB-5 --> (Figure 7(b); trained policies, 500 updates<!-- N:DX-40 -->, $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, Coupling A on). They are −6.87 and −10.66 points<!-- N:DX-13 --> for the grid's two seeds<!-- N:DX-40 --> and −4.70 points for a same-seed retrain<!-- N:DX-13 -->, each one run per cell<!-- N:DX-13 --> and without an interval. The grid's first estimate, the mean of its two seeds<!-- N:CB-6 -->, was −8.8 points<!-- N:CB-6 -->. The High survival floor, sd 6.21 points<!-- N:CB-7 -->, was measured later on four identical runs<!-- N:DX-40 --> of a separate artifact: a reference scale (cross-artifact). The first estimate is 1.41 times<!-- N:CB-5 --> this floor and the replicates' mean about 1.19 times<!-- N:CB-5 --> it. We report a consistent effect of modest size; the first analysis graded it "strong" against a two-seed<!-- N:DX-38 --> spread, a label retracted under the rule of §8.1. Completion is not resolved, since its contrast flipped sign on the same-seed retrain<!-- N:CB-8 -->.

At Medium, the severity of the held-out test configuration (β = 0.49<!-- N:CAL-3 -->), Coupling B's own survival effect in the same grid was −0.03 points<!-- N:CB-9 -->. It was judged against a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread, $\sigma_{\mathrm{seed}} = 0.72$ points<!-- N:CB-9 -->, with no measured floor; the Medium survival floors measured later, on two<!-- N:METH-1 --> GPUs, are 1.29 and 1.30 points<!-- N:METH-1 -->. Coupling B's measured survival cost is therefore at High, while §9's test runs at Medium (§10, item 1).

The masking concentrates on moments of danger, alive agent-steps with a Burning cell inside the agent's crop. In the $\kappa_B = 1.0$<!-- N:CB-1 --> arm the masked share of the crop at danger moments is 0.0809, 0.0560 and 0.2424<!-- N:DX-11 --> at Low, Medium and High, against 0.00068, 0.00249 and 0.01559<!-- N:DX-11 --> over all alive agent-steps: 118, 22 and 16 times<!-- N:CB-10 --> more concentrated (ratios of unrounded shares; Figure 7(a)). Within trained policies at 500 updates<!-- N:DX-40 --> the attenuation was not behaviourally suppressed: the coupled policies' masking ceiling, which does not depend on $\kappa_B$, sat at or above its random-policy value at every severity.

### 6.5 The co-active counter, and what it can see

The environment logs, at every step, the number of collapse-seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop, within Chebyshev radius 4<!-- N:DX-37 --> of its post-step position<!-- N:CO-4 -->. It was meant to catch both couplings at once, but only Coupling A produces those ignitions and $\kappa_B$ does not enter the count, so the counter is co-active only in all-on configurations. In a training mixture it reads the Coupling-A marginal alone, which is why the mediation analysis registered on it is void (§9.2).

{% include figure.html path="assets/img/submission/fig05_coactive.png" class="img-fluid" alt="Three bar charts, one per severity, of the share of episodes with zero, one, two, three, and four or more co-active events." caption="Figure 8. The co-active counter per episode, by severity." %}

<p class="caption">Share of episodes with each number of co-active events in the coupled arm of the Coupling-B calibration grid (labelled m44), seeds pooled; both couplings are on in every cell. The figure cannot show a floor, since none exists for this counter, or a training mixture (§6.5). <span class="figure-note">Status: descriptive; trained policies, 500 updates<!-- N:DX-40 -->; no floor · static.</span></p>

In these all-on cells the counter is rare and bursty (Figure 8). The share of episodes with no co-active event is 0.563, 0.578 and 0.883<!-- N:CO-2 --> at Low, Medium and High, the mean at Medium is 0.64<!-- N:CO-1 --> events per episode, and the variance-to-mean ratio, 1.31 to 1.40<!-- N:CO-3 -->, exceeds the Poisson value of 1. Low and Medium are indistinguishable and High is lower, because seeded ignitions fall while the near-agent share stays in its range (§5.3).

7.1 The kernel

Definition 7 (Communication denial, as implemented). After the agents move, the comms kernel \(T_K(k' \mid x')\) samples a directed link graph over the alive agents: for every ordered pair \(i \ne j\) of alive agents,

\[\Pr\big[\, i \to j \text{ open} \,\big] = \mathbf{1}\big[\lVert x'_i - x'_j \rVert_\infty \le R_{\mathrm{comm}}\big]\,(1 - \delta),\]

with \(R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 16\)COM-6 cells. Each agent emits a message \(m_i \in [-1, 1]^{8}\)DX-17 at step \(t\); at \(t + 1\) each receiver \(j\) gets the mean of the messages delivered over the links then realized, \(\bar m_j = \sum_i k'_{ij}\, m_i \,/\, \max(1, \sum_i k'_{ij})\), which is the zero vector when \(j\) is isolated. \(T_K\) reads positions and aliveness only, so denial is mechanistically independent of the fire. One uniform per ordered pair is drawn at every step whatever \(\delta\) is, so \(\delta = 0\) gives the deterministic range graph and \(\delta = 1\), the value locked on 2026-07-30DX-401, gives the empty graph, both bitwise. The theory's range-dependent link probability is implemented as this hard Chebyshev range.

Listing 6. Delivered messages carry no gradient.

hidden commentLISTING: che/train/ippo.py:323-329
# che/train/ippo.py:323-329 (verbatim)
        # Messages emitted this step are delivered next step. Nothing crosses
        # an episode boundary (autoreset), and stop_gradient states in code
        # what the batch-storage already implies: the channel is not a
        # differentiable path (Q3 ruling; networks.py docstring).
        messages = jax.lax.stop_gradient(
            jnp.where(done[:, None, None], 0.0, emitted)
        )

No gradient crosses the channel, so the message head, a tanh-bounded dense layer on the trunk, stays at its initializationENV-12: an agent emits a frozen random projection of trunk features that are themselves trained. The receivers' weights on the message inputs are trained, which makes message use testable; what to say was never learned.

7.2 When communication should matter

Remark 2′ refines a result of Pynadath & Tambe [35]. Free communication lowers the complexity of the team decision problem when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ concerns the value of communication under collective partial observability.

Remark 2′. Extend \(E_{2C}\) with a second agent. (i) Under a team-any reward, communication has zero marginal value when the agents are interchangeable, expendable (no death cost) and at least as numerous as the hypotheses: coverage then achieves \(J = 1\) under total denial. When any qualifier fails, value returns; in general it scales with the deficit of agents relative to hypotheses, and a death cost, such as CHE's \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, prices the redundancy that substitution spends. (ii) In the courier variant, where only agent 1 can complete the task and it observes the other agent only through messages, the denied optimum within the no-idle policy class is \(\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B)\); with slack \(\ell_f\), optimal denied play buys information by idling at the branch and achieves \(\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 \tilde q(\kappa_B)\), where \(\tilde q\) is the probability of being informed by the commitment point under the best idling schedule, with \(\tilde q \ge q\) (equality iff \(\ell_f = 0\)), so

\[\mathrm{VoC}_{\mathrm{true}}(\kappa_B) = \tfrac12\big(1 - \tilde q(\kappa_B)\big) \le \tfrac12\big(1 - q(\kappa_B)\big).\]

Both \(q\) and \(\tilde q\) decrease in \(\kappa_B\), so the value of communication increases with perception decay.

We ran the courier variant with scripted policies, delivering messages through the production link kernel and computing \(\tau\) with the shared transmittance function, which tests both kernels. The free arm always succeedsDX-15, the no-idle denied arm matches \(\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q\), and the value of communication against that arm, \(\mathrm{VoC}_{\mathrm{gated}} = \tfrac12(1 - q)\), rises monotonically from 0 to 0.498COM-1 (Figure 9(a); Appendix A).

7.3 In CHE the channel carries nothing measurable

CHE's agents meet one of Remark 2′(i)'s qualifiers: they are twelveENV-6 interchangeable agents sharing one set of parametersENV-11. Nothing establishes that they are at least as numerous as the hypotheses, and their deaths are priced (\(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1), so the clean-zero conditions are not fully met.

In a content ablation at Medium (\(\delta = 0\), \(R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 8\)COM-2, two seedsCOM-2, 500 updatesDX-305), three arms of identical architecture pass live, zeroed or shuffled messages (the delivered aggregate set to zero with links kept; sender identities permuted within a step). No pair differs beyond a bar of twiceCOM-2 the earlier Medium floor, about 3 pointsCOM-2. The same ablation at High (three seedsDX-16, \(R_{\mathrm{comm}}\) = 8 and 16DX-16) found that raising the mean alive out-degree from 1.01 to 2.99COM-3 changed nothing, ruling out "nobody was in range". The wider High floors put its bars at 10.44V4-3 (completion) and 12.43 pointsV4-3 (survival).

In the denial study (Medium, four seedsCOM-4, \(R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 16\)COM-4, message path live in both arms), completion is 0.38 pointsCOM-4 higher and survival 1.43 pointsCOM-4 lower with links open (\(\delta = 0\)) than with links denied (\(\delta = 1\)), against bars of 7.99COM-4 and 2.60 pointsCOM-4. Both bars are twiceV4-2 the floor sd measured on the GPU that ran the study (Figure 9(b–d)). Every bar here is a post-hoc floor grading that a true effect of its size would clear only about half the timeV4-1 (§8.2), so none of these results is a point null. A further condition, on danger-moment outcomes, is VOID: its threshold, chosen at implementation, sat below the quantity's own run-to-run nondeterminism (§8.1).

The content ablations supply a mechanism: in the live arm a receiver can tell that it heard from someone without decoding anything; in the zeroed arm it cannot. That signal needs no encoder and went unused at both severities and, at High, at both ranges. The neglect is therefore on the demand side (the agents did not need the information) rather than the channel side (they could not read it), and the frozen encoder cannot account for it.

Because deaths are priced, Remark 2′(i) leaves communication some value here, which the measurements find weak at this scale and this death price. Gradient-shaped messaging is untested, and a bound measured on a frozen random encoder does not strictly bound a trained one. We claim only that communication denial is inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale.

The composition §9 tests is, in effect, Coupling A × Coupling B, with \(\delta = 1.0\)COM-6 kept in \(\theta^*\) for fidelity to the registration. The δ-only thirdDES-8 of ISO's training is fire-only, and §9.1 gives the consequence.

### 7.1 The kernel

**Definition 7 (Communication denial, as implemented).** After the agents move, the comms kernel $T_K(k' \mid x')$ samples a directed link graph over the alive agents: for every ordered pair $i \ne j$ of alive agents,

$$
\Pr\big[\, i \to j \text{ open} \,\big] = \mathbf{1}\big[\lVert x'_i - x'_j \rVert_\infty \le R_{\mathrm{comm}}\big]\,(1 - \delta),
$$

with $R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 16$<!-- N:COM-6 --> cells. Each agent emits a message $m_i \in [-1, 1]^{8}$<!-- N:DX-17 --> at step $t$; at $t + 1$ each receiver $j$ gets the mean of the messages delivered over the links then realized, $\bar m_j = \sum_i k'_{ij}\, m_i \,/\, \max(1, \sum_i k'_{ij})$, which is the zero vector when $j$ is isolated. $T_K$ reads positions and aliveness only, so denial is mechanistically independent of the fire. One uniform per ordered pair is drawn at every step whatever $\delta$ is, so $\delta = 0$ gives the deterministic range graph and $\delta = 1$, the value locked on 2026-07-30<!-- N:DX-401 -->, gives the empty graph, both bitwise. The theory's range-dependent link probability is implemented as this hard Chebyshev range.

**Listing 6.** Delivered messages carry no gradient.

<!-- LISTING: che/train/ippo.py:323-329 -->
```python
# che/train/ippo.py:323-329 (verbatim)
        # Messages emitted this step are delivered next step. Nothing crosses
        # an episode boundary (autoreset), and stop_gradient states in code
        # what the batch-storage already implies: the channel is not a
        # differentiable path (Q3 ruling; networks.py docstring).
        messages = jax.lax.stop_gradient(
            jnp.where(done[:, None, None], 0.0, emitted)
        )
```

No gradient crosses the channel, so the message head, a tanh-bounded dense layer on the trunk, stays at its initialization<!-- N:ENV-12 -->: an agent emits a frozen random projection of trunk features that are themselves trained. The receivers' weights on the message inputs are trained, which makes message use testable; what to say was never learned.

### 7.2 When communication should matter

Remark 2′ refines a result of Pynadath & Tambe <d-cite key="pynadath2002"></d-cite>. Free communication lowers the complexity of the team decision problem when the world is collectively observable (their Theorem 4 and Table 2), and makes no difference when it is individually observable or not observable at all (their Theorems 5–6). Remark 2′ concerns the value of communication under collective partial observability.

**Remark 2′.** Extend $E_{2C}$ with a second agent. (i) Under a team-any reward, communication has zero marginal value when the agents are interchangeable, expendable (no death cost) and at least as numerous as the hypotheses: coverage then achieves $J = 1$ under total denial. When any qualifier fails, value returns; in general it scales with the deficit of agents relative to hypotheses, and a death cost, such as CHE's $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, prices the redundancy that substitution spends. (ii) In the courier variant, where only agent 1 can complete the task and it observes the other agent only through messages, the denied optimum within the no-idle policy class is $\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q(\kappa_B)$; with slack $\ell_f$, optimal denied play buys information by idling at the branch and achieves $\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 \tilde q(\kappa_B)$, where $\tilde q$ is the probability of being informed by the commitment point under the best idling schedule, with $\tilde q \ge q$ (equality iff $\ell_f = 0$), so

$$
\mathrm{VoC}_{\mathrm{true}}(\kappa_B) = \tfrac12\big(1 - \tilde q(\kappa_B)\big) \le \tfrac12\big(1 - q(\kappa_B)\big).
$$

Both $q$ and $\tilde q$ decrease in $\kappa_B$, so the value of communication increases with perception decay.

We ran the courier variant with scripted policies, delivering messages through the production link kernel and computing $\tau$ with the shared transmittance function, which tests both kernels. The free arm always succeeds<!-- N:DX-15 -->, the no-idle denied arm matches $\tfrac12 + \tfrac12 q$, and the value of communication against that arm, $\mathrm{VoC}_{\mathrm{gated}} = \tfrac12(1 - q)$, rises monotonically from 0 to 0.498<!-- N:COM-1 --> (Figure 9(a); Appendix A).

### 7.3 In CHE the channel carries nothing measurable

CHE's agents meet one of Remark 2′(i)'s qualifiers: they are twelve<!-- N:ENV-6 --> interchangeable agents sharing one set of parameters<!-- N:ENV-11 -->. Nothing establishes that they are at least as numerous as the hypotheses, and their deaths are priced ($d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->), so the clean-zero conditions are not fully met.

In a content ablation at Medium ($\delta = 0$, $R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 8$<!-- N:COM-2 -->, two seeds<!-- N:COM-2 -->, 500 updates<!-- N:DX-305 -->), three arms of identical architecture pass live, zeroed or shuffled messages (the delivered aggregate set to zero with links kept; sender identities permuted within a step). No pair differs beyond a bar of twice<!-- N:COM-2 --> the earlier Medium floor, about 3 points<!-- N:COM-2 -->. The same ablation at High (three seeds<!-- N:DX-16 -->, $R_{\mathrm{comm}}$ = 8 and 16<!-- N:DX-16 -->) found that raising the mean alive out-degree from 1.01 to 2.99<!-- N:COM-3 --> changed nothing, ruling out "nobody was in range". The wider High floors put its bars at 10.44<!-- N:V4-3 --> (completion) and 12.43 points<!-- N:V4-3 --> (survival).

In the denial study (Medium, four seeds<!-- N:COM-4 -->, $R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 16$<!-- N:COM-4 -->, message path live in both arms), completion is 0.38 points<!-- N:COM-4 --> higher and survival 1.43 points<!-- N:COM-4 --> lower with links open ($\delta = 0$) than with links denied ($\delta = 1$), against bars of 7.99<!-- N:COM-4 --> and 2.60 points<!-- N:COM-4 -->. Both bars are twice<!-- N:V4-2 --> the floor sd measured on the GPU that ran the study (Figure 9(b–d)). Every bar here is a post-hoc floor grading that a true effect of its size would clear only about half the time<!-- N:V4-1 --> (§8.2), so none of these results is a point null. A further condition, on danger-moment outcomes, is VOID: its threshold, chosen at implementation, sat below the quantity's own run-to-run nondeterminism (§8.1).

The content ablations supply a mechanism: in the live arm a receiver can tell that it heard from someone without decoding anything; in the zeroed arm it cannot. That signal needs no encoder and went unused at both severities and, at High, at both ranges. The neglect is therefore on the demand side (the agents did not need the information) rather than the channel side (they could not read it), and the frozen encoder cannot account for it.

Because deaths are priced, Remark 2′(i) leaves communication some value here, which the measurements find weak at this scale and this death price. Gradient-shaped messaging is untested, and a bound measured on a frozen random encoder does not strictly bound a trained one. We claim only that communication denial is inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale.

The composition §9 tests is, in effect, Coupling A × Coupling B, with $\delta = 1.0$<!-- N:COM-6 --> kept in $\theta^*$ for fidelity to the registration. The δ-only third<!-- N:DES-8 --> of ISO's training is fire-only, and §9.1 gives the consequence.

8 Methodology: bars come with floors

GPU training is not reproducible run to run: the compiler chooses kernels by timing them and some reductions use atomics. A reproducibility floor is the standard deviation of a metric over identical reruns, with the same seed, configuration and code. Each rule below came from a defect in the project's record; Appendix C lists every floor.

8.1 A bar without a floor is void

No acceptance threshold enters an analysis script without a measured floor for the quantity it grades, or an explicit UNDERPOWERED flag. A threshold below its instrument's noise cannot pass under the null, so its output is void, and a void test voids a PASS as it voids a FAIL. The rule followed fourV5-1 bars with invented constants (Appendix C). In one, the denial study of §7 graded a condition with a 20 %COM-5 threshold, chosen at implementation, against a quantity whose rerun nondeterminism is 27.2 %COM-5. Since the threshold sits below the noise, the condition is VOID; the observed 23.2 %COM-5 exceeded the threshold but not the noise.

Floors are facts per metric and per GPU. Re-measuring the Medium floor moved completion's 2.75-foldMETH-1 and left survival's unchanged, 0.0129METH-1 against 0.0130METH-1. The two studies differ in GPU, code version and configuration, so the exhibit cannot say which of the three moved the completion floor. The floor is measured on the GPU that runs the grid.

Floors are facts per artifact. In a refactor of the environment code, the rerun floor measured on the reference version read zero, so twoMETH-2 diagnostic channels that differed between the versions looked like a semantic change. The refactored version then differed from itself 1 time in 4METH-2 on the same channels. An equivalence claim between A and B needs A against A and B against B as well as A against B.

8.2 Contrasts and power

A contrast is graded on its own standard error. For Γ, the difference between the JOINT and ISO arm means over \(k_{\mathrm{JOINT}}\) and \(k_{\mathrm{ISO}}\) seeds with seed standard deviations \(s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}\) and \(s_{\mathrm{ISO}}\),

\[\operatorname{sd}(\Gamma) = \sqrt{\frac{s_{\mathrm{ISO}}^2}{k_{\mathrm{ISO}}} + \frac{s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}^2}{k_{\mathrm{JOINT}}}},\]

which for equal \(k\) is \(\sqrt{(s_{\mathrm{ISO}}^2 + s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}^2)/k}\). The per-arm forms, each arm's own \(s\sqrt{2/k}\), bracket that value without equalling it: at \(k = 34\)METH-3, on the 1000DX-34-update completion floors and a 0.03DES-4 effect, ISO's gave 92.1 %METH-3 power and JOINT's 62.8 %METH-3, where the contrast's power was 76.7 %METH-3.

A design-stage power statement is the minimum detectable effect at 80 %METH-7 power and the family-corrected level,

\[\mathrm{MDE}_{80} = \big(z_\alpha + z_{0.8}\big)\,\operatorname{sd}(\Gamma),\]

with \(z_\alpha = 2.2365\)DES-13 the Šidák-corrected critical value for the two co-primaries. The exclusion bound \(X\) that §9 attaches to a null is this quantity on the grid's realized seed dispersion. A bare \(2\sigma\sqrt{2/k}\) is the effect at which an uncorrected test would just reject, which it detects about halfV4-1 the time; it survives only for grading an observed effect against a floor after the fact, labelled as such. The confirmatory seed count was first ruled at \(k = 20\)METH-4 on such a figure, without the Šidák correction; corrected, completion had 55.6 %METH-4 power against its motivating effect band, a slip caught before any run.

8.3 Floors move with the run, and gates must not divide by them

Floors grow with run length. From 500DX-34 to 1000DX-34 updates the completion floor grew 2.1-fold for ISO and 5.2-fold for JOINTMETH-5, with length confounded with machine.

A gate that divides by a floor diverges on more reproducible hardware. A registered convergence check, retired before the grid, divided each arm's late-training drift by its floor, so any nonzero drift fails as the floor goes to zero; it fired when JOINT's floor fell faster than its drift. The \(T^*\) ruling of 2026-08-11METH-6 retired it as a gate, fixed the estimand as Γ at matched compute, and made Γ(t) required robustness evidence (§9.2).

8.4 The floor is a check, not the test variance

The floor and the test statistic are different variances. A floor comes from identical reruns at one seed, while a \(k\)-seed arm mean varies by seed as well. The confirmatory contrast is graded on the grid's own seed dispersion. The floor serves the check that an effect exceeds rerun noise, which uses the floor-based standard error of Γ: the display in §8.2 with floors in place of seed standard deviations (§9.2). For design-stage power, the floor gives an upper bound, labelled as one.

8.5 Instruments state what they are blind to

A differenced test sees asymmetry and nothing else. A condition of the form "no cross-arm difference in X" cancels any X present in both arms. In 24 of 24DX-32 rendered episodes of the Coupling-B calibration grid, the alive agents' centroid drifted towards a wall (rendered-pixel centroids, a diagnostic). That grid's inertness falsifier, logged before its data, included the condition "no cross-arm exposure/positioning difference", which could not see a drift present in both arms; the verdict it fed rests on its other conditions.

Measuring is not grading. A recorded channel becomes evidence only inside a registered analysis family, with a floor measured on its artifact. Position-drift diagnostics added before the grid are recorded for every grid run. They are recorded, not graded: adding them to the registered family after registration would have enlarged it and inflated its correction.

The differential drift between the arms, bias (b) of §9.1, changed sign between the fourDES-10V6-4 floor studies that read it at 1000DES-13 updates before unblinding, so §9.1 reports every reading.

8.6 Where this paper falls short of its own rules

Coupling B's Medium survival effect was judged only against a three-seedCB-9 spread (§6.4). The trained-policy contrasts of §5 and §6 come from two calibration grids without identical reruns: their spreads are seed ranges, and any floor shown beside them is a reference scale (cross-artifact). In the secondary design, floors exist only for the sweep arm at \(p = 0.5\)DX-28; the intermediate sweep arms assume that floor, and the identification arm is ungradedDX-306. ISO-4 has no identical reruns, so the contrasts that involve it are graded on seed dispersion alone, and the High-severity readout flags ISO-4 UNDERPOWERED for the check against rerun noise. The position-drift diagnostics have no floor. Every floor is itself a point estimate: measured on fourDX-303 identical runs, a standard deviation is uncertain by roughly ±40 %DX-303.

### 8.2 Contrasts and power

A contrast is graded on its own standard error. For Γ, the difference between the JOINT and ISO arm means over $k_{\mathrm{JOINT}}$ and $k_{\mathrm{ISO}}$ seeds with seed standard deviations $s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}$ and $s_{\mathrm{ISO}}$,

$$
\operatorname{sd}(\Gamma) = \sqrt{\frac{s_{\mathrm{ISO}}^2}{k_{\mathrm{ISO}}} + \frac{s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}^2}{k_{\mathrm{JOINT}}}},
$$

which for equal $k$ is $\sqrt{(s_{\mathrm{ISO}}^2 + s_{\mathrm{JOINT}}^2)/k}$. The per-arm forms, each arm's own $s\sqrt{2/k}$, bracket that value without equalling it: at $k = 34$<!-- N:METH-3 -->, on the 1000<!-- N:DX-34 -->-update completion floors and a 0.03<!-- N:DES-4 --> effect, ISO's gave 92.1 %<!-- N:METH-3 --> power and JOINT's 62.8 %<!-- N:METH-3 -->, where the contrast's power was 76.7 %<!-- N:METH-3 -->.

A design-stage power statement is the minimum detectable effect at 80 %<!-- N:METH-7 --> power and the family-corrected level,

$$
\mathrm{MDE}_{80} = \big(z_\alpha + z_{0.8}\big)\,\operatorname{sd}(\Gamma),
$$

with $z_\alpha = 2.2365$<!-- N:DES-13 --> the Šidák-corrected critical value for the two co-primaries. The exclusion bound $X$ that §9 attaches to a null is this quantity on the grid's realized seed dispersion. A bare $2\sigma\sqrt{2/k}$ is the effect at which an uncorrected test would just reject, which it detects about half<!-- N:V4-1 --> the time; it survives only for grading an observed effect against a floor after the fact, labelled as such. The confirmatory seed count was first ruled at $k = 20$<!-- N:METH-4 --> on such a figure, without the Šidák correction; corrected, completion had 55.6 %<!-- N:METH-4 --> power against its motivating effect band, a slip caught before any run.

### 8.3 Floors move with the run, and gates must not divide by them

Floors grow with run length. From 500<!-- N:DX-34 --> to 1000<!-- N:DX-34 --> updates the completion floor grew 2.1-fold for ISO and 5.2-fold for JOINT<!-- N:METH-5 -->, with length confounded with machine.

A gate that divides by a floor diverges on more reproducible hardware. A registered convergence check, retired before the grid, divided each arm's late-training drift by its floor, so any nonzero drift fails as the floor goes to zero; it fired when JOINT's floor fell faster than its drift. The $T^*$ ruling of 2026-08-11<!-- N:METH-6 --> retired it as a gate, fixed the estimand as Γ at matched compute, and made Γ(t) required robustness evidence (§9.2).

### 8.4 The floor is a check, not the test variance

The floor and the test statistic are different variances. A floor comes from identical reruns at one seed, while a $k$-seed arm mean varies by seed as well. The confirmatory contrast is graded on the grid's own seed dispersion. The floor serves the check that an effect exceeds rerun noise, which uses the floor-based standard error of Γ: the display in §8.2 with floors in place of seed standard deviations (§9.2). For design-stage power, the floor gives an upper bound, labelled as one.

### 8.5 Instruments state what they are blind to

A differenced test sees asymmetry and nothing else. A condition of the form "no cross-arm difference in X" cancels any X present in both arms. In 24 of 24<!-- N:DX-32 --> rendered episodes of the Coupling-B calibration grid, the alive agents' centroid drifted towards a wall (rendered-pixel centroids, a diagnostic). That grid's inertness falsifier, logged before its data, included the condition "no cross-arm exposure/positioning difference", which could not see a drift present in both arms; the verdict it fed rests on its other conditions.

Measuring is not grading. A recorded channel becomes evidence only inside a registered analysis family, with a floor measured on its artifact. Position-drift diagnostics added before the grid are recorded for every grid run. They are recorded, not graded: adding them to the registered family after registration would have enlarged it and inflated its correction.

The differential drift between the arms, bias (b) of §9.1, changed sign between the four<!-- N:DES-10 --><!-- N:V6-4 --> floor studies that read it at 1000<!-- N:DES-13 --> updates before unblinding, so §9.1 reports every reading.

### 8.6 Where this paper falls short of its own rules

Coupling B's Medium survival effect was judged only against a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread (§6.4). The trained-policy contrasts of §5 and §6 come from two calibration grids without identical reruns: their spreads are seed ranges, and any floor shown beside them is a reference scale (cross-artifact). In the secondary design, floors exist only for the sweep arm at $p = 0.5$<!-- N:DX-28 -->; the intermediate sweep arms assume that floor, and the identification arm is ungraded<!-- N:DX-306 -->. ISO-4 has no identical reruns, so the contrasts that involve it are graded on seed dispersion alone, and the High-severity readout flags ISO-4 UNDERPOWERED for the check against rerun noise. The position-drift diagnostics have no floor. Every floor is itself a point estimate: measured on four<!-- N:DX-303 --> identical runs, a standard deviation is uncertain by roughly ±40 %<!-- N:DX-303 -->.

9.1 Design

Definition 8 (Training protocols and the compositional gap). Let \(J_\theta(\pi)\) be the expected episodic return of joint policy \(\pi\) in \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\), and let \(\theta^*\) be the test configuration, every stressor on at a severity held out of training. Let \(\Theta_{\mathrm{iso}}\) be the configurations in which at most one of \(\kappa_A\), \(\kappa_B\), \(\delta\) is nonzero; the fire (\(\beta > 0\)) burns in all of them. The ISO protocol trains one policy \(\pi_{\mathrm{iso}}\) on a uniform mixture of configurations in \(\Theta_{\mathrm{iso}}\). The JOINT protocol trains one policy \(\pi_{\mathrm{joint}}\) on a mixture whose support includes configurations with every stressor on, at training severities only. The compositional gap is

\[\Gamma(\theta^*) = J_{\theta^*}(\pi_{\mathrm{joint}}) - J_{\theta^*}(\pi_{\mathrm{iso}}).\]

The registered test replaces \(J\) by an outcome metric's mean over evaluation episodes, on two co-primaries: task completion, the founding primary, and survival rate (§3.5). Survival became a co-primary on 2026-08-02DES-12, before the grid, because, as that ruling records, both couplings' calibration grids had moved survival while their completion effects sat at or below the floors then measured (§5–§6).

Proposition 4 (A transfer bound for a fixed policy). For a fixed policy and two configurations that share the reward, the difference between its discounted returns is bounded above by a term that grows with how far their transition and observation kernels differ. Every configuration in \(\Theta_{\mathrm{iso}}\) differs from \(\theta^*\) on the states where both couplings act at once, a collapse-seeded fire within perception-attenuation range of an agent (proof idea in Appendix A).

Proposition 4 bounds what ISO can be guaranteed to transfer, not what happens. It does not lower-bound Γ, and compositional generalization can emerge from isolated training; it motivates the experiment, and the experiment decides. Its prediction about ISO's exposure to that region was not tested, because the counter built to measure visits there (§6.5) is co-active only in all-on configurations (§9.2).

The test configuration θ* has every stressor on at the held-out severity, β 0.49, \(\kappa_A\) 0.06, \(\kappa_B\) 1.0, δ 1.0CAL-3CA-1CB-1COM-6, and no arm trains at β 0.49CAL-3 (Figure 2 in §1). In the table, μ is the per-coupling frequency, the share of training episodes carrying a given coupling, and p the co-occurrence, the share carrying both. The active share is the share with at least one behaviourally active stressor, and the fire-only share ν, counted behaviourally, is the rest.

arm components, each at β 0.43 and 0.70CAL-3 μ p active share fire-only ν
ISO A-only, B-only, δ-only; 6 × 1/6DES-16 1/3DES-7 0DES-7 2/3DES-14 1/3DES-8
ISO-4 A-only, B-only; 4 × 1/4DES-16 1/2DES-14 0DES-14 1DES-14 0DES-14
JOINT all-on; 2 × 1/2DES-16 1DES-7 1DES-7 1DES-14 0DES-14

ISO-4, a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family, is ISO without its two δ-only components, and the corrected gap Γ₄ is JOINT − ISO-4. All arms share the network and the IPPO hyperparameters (Appendix BDX-2), one shared-parameter policy per training seedENV-11[11], a budget of T* = 1000DES-13 updates of 32,768 environment steps eachDX-3, and one evaluation draw, seed 0 × 512 episodes at θ*DES-13.

Γ is read at matched compute, at the fixed budget T*METH-6, and convergence is claimed nowhere. The confirmatory family is the two co-primary tests, Šidák-corrected (m = 2DES-13): unpaired normal z tests against \(z_\alpha = 2.2365\)DES-13 on the contrast's standard error from each arm's seed dispersion (§8.2). A null carries its exclusion bound X, the 80 %-power MDE at that αMETH-7. Positive cross-arm covariance, the expected sign given the shared seed integers, makes the unpaired test conservative. Floors serve only the check that an effect exceeds rerun noise (§8.4) and, as an upper bound, design-stage power (Appendix D); every interval is conditional on the one evaluation draw.

The design registered k = 40DES-1 seeds per arm and a seed rule, capped at 60DES-1, that recomputes k from floors re-measured on the grid's GPU. It gave 46DES-2 on an RTX PRO 6000 and 72DES-3 on GPU 1, the RTX 5090 that ran the rest of the grid and every run in the confirmatory artifact (GPU 2 and GPU 3 are two more of that modelDX-5; Appendix F). Raising the cap to 72 on 2026-09-22DES-3 was a deviation from a registered rule, recorded as one, made before unblinding, and conservative, since added seeds favour neither sign of Γ. The registered k = 60 analysis, on seeds 1–60DES-1RES-3, is reported beside it; realized completion power at a 0.03DES-4 effect is 60.2 % and 71.6 % at k = 60 and 72DES-6.

JOINT sees each coupling three times as often as ISODES-7, so Γ mixes per-coupling frequency with composition. A secondary sweep holds μ = 0.5 while p moves from 0 to 0.5DX-28, in the spirit of DBCA[12], which the ISO and JOINT endpoints do not follow. On the triangle of training mixtures \(\mu = (1 + p - \nu)/2\), so a fixed μ forces ν = pDES-9. Identification arms on the parallel line μ = 0.4, ν = 0.2 + pDES-9 identify a plane in p and ν (Appendix H). Secondary arms run k = 20DES-13 seeds, with δ off, on GPU 1DX-5. The p = 0DX-28 sweep arm therefore trains on ISO-4's mixture on JOINT's GPU, and JOINT, unlike every secondary arm, carries δ = 1.0COM-6, inert (§7). The secondary design is non-verdict-bearing.

The registered design contains an error, and the bias it creates inflates Γ. Communication denial had been found inert on 2026-07-30V6-2 (§7). The design registered on 2026-08-02V6-3 kept δ in θ* for fidelity to the registration and excluded it from the secondary sweeps because it was inert. It still gave ISO two δ-only components of sixV6-3. A question it set for reviewers judged the arms symmetric on δ, on the premise that neither trains on δ = 1.0COM-6, which is false for both, and asked for a second opinion. So a third of ISO's training episodesDES-8 are fire-only, which the design never counted: ISO's configured no-stressor share of 0DES-16 is true nominally and false behaviourally, and JOINT's active share is 1.5 times ISO'sDX-31. A review of the registered design identified this on 2026-08-16V6-1, before the grid, noting that the confirmatory arm applied a different rule to the same fact as the sweeps. The grid began on 2026-09-09DES-12 without the correction. The bias, its direction and ISO-4 with its reading rule were registered on 2026-09-18DES-15, mid-grid (120 of 252 runsDES-15) and before unblinding, on our attestation, recorded with the ruling, that no outcome mean had been computed or viewed. The composition actually tested is A × B.

Bias (b) is the registered caveat on differential drift: JOINT − ISO completion drift over the last 100 of 1000DES-13 training updatesDES-10, a local sensitivity estimate and never a bound. The fourDES-10V6-4 floor studies that read it at 1000DES-13 updates gave +0.0242V6-4 per 100 updates on the confirmatory arms' floor study, +0.01209DES-10 on the grid's launch floor study (2026-08-10DES-5) and +0.0179DES-10 on the 2026-09-08 re-measured floor, all three on an RTX PRO 6000, but −0.0083DES-10 on GPU 1. In the 2000RES-15-update subsample (T = 2000 in the registration; §9.2), the differential training-log slopes over updates 500–1000 are +0.00247 (completion) and +0.00718 (survival) per 100 updatesDES-11. The sign reverses on the grid GPU, and no direction is claimed.

The registered outcomes, branches A–D of 2026-08-13DES-12 and a fifthDX-405 cell, \(A_c\), added before unblinding, are in Appendix E. Beyond a null completion contrast and a positive survival contrast, branch B needs a registered falsifier to pass. The ISO-4 rule states that if Γ rejects on a co-primary and "Γ₄'s CI on that co-primary includes 0 or its sign is opposite, the paper states that the effect does not survive the inert-share correction". If Γ and Γ₄ are both null, "Γ₄ is quoted as a second exclusion bound". Appendix D quotes every rule with its date.

The grid ended on 2026-09-22DES-12; its runner computes no cross-arm quantity. On 2026-09-26DX-405 the pre-unblind rulings fixed the statistic, the sign rule, k = 72DES-3 as primary and \(A_c\). Unblinding, the first computation of any outcome contrast, followed the same dayDES-12 (Appendix F).

9.2 Results

At k = 72DES-3, Γ rejects on survival and is null on completion (Figure 11 and the contrast table). The completion null excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66 pointsRES-1, the upper end of its interval. Its exclusion bound X, the gap of either sign the design would detect with 80 %METH-7 power, is 3.29 pointsRES-1, and the negative point estimate does not reject. The registered k = 60DES-1RES-3 analysis reads the same and is flagged UNDERPOWERED.

replicate difference: p = 0DX-28 sweep arm − ISO-4 20 / 20DES-13 +1.21 [−2.79, +5.20], 95 %DX-19 −0.15 [−0.86, +0.56], 95 %RES-9 secondary, across GPUs
\(\Gamma_H\), all-on at β = 0.70CAL-3 72 / 72DES-3 null: −0.79RES-14 [−3.26, +1.68]DX-26 null: +0.38RES-14 [−1.46, +2.23]DX-26 evaluation only, out of family, not held out
\(\Gamma_{H,4}\) = JOINT − ISO-4 at β = 0.70CAL-3 72 / 20DES-3DES-13 null: −0.75 [−3.87, +2.36]DX-26 null: −1.28 [−3.50, +0.95]DX-26 no reading rule of its own
